{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.82999pt}{-2.85004pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{\resizebox{655360}{}{\!\!\!\!
\hbox to11.19pt{\vbox to7.88pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.35997pt\lower-5.18315pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.46 -3.46 L 3.46 3.46 L -8.52 0 L 3.46 -3.46 L 3.46 3.46}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.46 -2.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.5pt}{-1.60004pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.53 -0.48}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.82999pt}{-0.35004pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.53 -3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.82999pt}{-2.85004pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\!\cdots=\prod_{\mathsf{x}}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\,.

(31)

All
\overleftarrow{X}
-operators in the one-body term commute with
\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{\textsf{(global)}}
,
and the two-body term [recalling the Weyl-type relations (
15
)]
transforms as

\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{\textsf{(global)}}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{\dagger\textsf{(global)}}=\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}(g^{-1}).\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}(g).\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}=\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\,,

(32)

showing that the symmetry holds for arbitrary
\{\text{C}_{\lambda},\text{f}_{g}\}
.
Because this symmetry stems from a non-local excitation constraint on the quantum-double bulk, only symmetric operators can be considered as
physical
in this context. The bulk ground-state constraint translates to a restriction of the
flux ladder to the collective subspace where
\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{\textsf{(global)}}=1
for all
h
.
We consider more general bulk-excitation conditions in Appx.
A
.

3.5
Gapped edges

Quantum double models admit gapped edges, which are classified by a subgroup
\textnormal{{K}}\subseteq\textnormal{{G}}
and its representations, which can be linear or projective
[
62
]
. In a projective representation, the group’s multiplication table is decorated
with phases in a way that is consistent with associativity. We consider only linear representations of
K
in this work, but an extension to the projective case should be straightforward.

A gapped edge described by
K
corresponds to the following instance of the flux ladder, written in terms of either
ZZ
-type operators or projections using Eqs. (
21
) and (
29
),

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{K}}\subseteq\textnormal{{G}}}^{\text{edge}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\mathsf{x}}\,\,\sum_{\lambda\in\text{irr}(\textnormal{{G}})}{\textstyle\frac{d_{\lambda}}{|\textnormal{{G}}|/|\textnormal{{K}}|}}\textsc{Tr}\big[\varPi_{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\textnormal{{K}}}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\big]+{\textstyle\frac{1}{\left|\textnormal{{K}}\right|}}\sum_{k\in\textnormal{{K}}}\overleftarrow{X}_{k}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\mathsf{x}}\,\,{\textstyle\frac{1}{\left|\textnormal{{K}}\right|}}\sum_{k\in\textnormal{{K}}}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{k}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+1\right)}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{\left|\textnormal{{K}}\right|}}\sum_{k\in\textnormal{{K}}}\overleftarrow{X}_{k}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}~,

(33e)

where
\varPi_{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\textnormal{{K}}}={\textstyle\frac{1}{\left|\textnormal{{K}}\right|}}\sum_{k\in\textnormal{{K}}}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)
(
69
),
and
\left|\textnormal{{K}}\right|
is the number of elements in
K
.

Apart from the
\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{\textsf{(global)}}
-symmetry inherited from the general flux ladder (
27
), these particular cases are also symmetric under
\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{\textsf{(global)}}
for
any
b\in\textnormal{{N}}(\textnormal{{K}})
— the
normalizer
of
K
in
G
.
The normalizer consists of any elements that permute elements of
K

under conjugation, and can include elements outside of
K
. Using the definition of
\overleftarrow{\varPi}
(
28
) and changing the summation index,

\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{\textsf{(global)}}\left(\sum_{k\in\textnormal{{K}}}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{k}^{(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+1)}\right)\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{\dagger\textsf{(global)}}=\sum_{k\in\textnormal{{K}}}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{bkb^{-1}}^{(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+1)}=\sum_{k\in\textnormal{{K}}}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{k}^{(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+1)}~.

(34)

Similar logic implies that the one-body sum is
likewise invariant, and that the two-body sum commutes
with each one-body term. In fact, this Hamiltonian can be made into a commuting projector model.

When treated as a standalone spin chain, the above Hamiltonian is a paradigmatic example of symmetry breaking
of
G
into
K
— a generalization of similar models for
abelian cases
[
110
,
111
,
112
]
.
Two notable extreme cases are

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{\langle 1\rangle\subseteq\textnormal{{G}}}^{\text{edge}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\mathsf{x}}\,\,\sum_{\lambda\in\text{irr}(\textnormal{{G}})}{\textstyle\frac{d_{\lambda}}{|\textnormal{{G}}|}}\textsc{Tr}\big[\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\big]=-\sum_{\mathsf{x}}\,\,{\textstyle\frac{1}{\left|\textnormal{{G}}\right|}}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{1}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+1\right)}

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{G}}\subseteq\textnormal{{G}}}^{\text{edge}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\mathsf{x}}\,\,{\textstyle\frac{1}{\left|\textnormal{{G}}\right|}}\sum_{g\in\textnormal{{G}}}\overleftarrow{X}_{g}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}~,

(35e)

up to identity terms. The case
\textnormal{{K}}=\left\langle 1\right\rangle
yields a
G
-ordered phase: the one-body terms become trivial,
and the
|\textnormal{{G}}|
ground states are tensor products
\cdots|g\rangle|g\rangle|g\rangle\cdots
of the same group element
g
. Order parameters distinguishing the
|\textnormal{{G}}|
symmetry-broken states are functions of

–

\widehat{Z}

(e.g.,
\textsc{Tr}[\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}]
), generalizing the famous
\widehat{\sigma}_{z}
order parameter for
\textnormal{{G}}=\textnormal{{Z}}_{2}
. The case
\textnormal{{K}}=\textnormal{{G}}
yields a disordered phase: the two-body
ZZ
-type term becomes trivial, and there is a unique product ground state with
|\textnormal{{G}}\rangle\equiv\frac{1}{|\textnormal{{G}}|}\sum_{g\in\textnormal{{G}}}|g\rangle
at each site. In the inbetween cases, the two-body term is minimized
at those states
|g,h\rangle

that satisfy
g^{-1}h\in\textnormal{{K}}
.
The one-body term [see Eq. (
91
)] projects onto
\textnormal{{G}}/\textnormal{{K}}
— the space of coset or
quotient states

|a\textnormal{{K}}\rangle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\left|\textnormal{{K}}\right|}}\sum_{k\in\textnormal{{K}}}|ak\rangle\,.

(36)

Both terms are jointly minimized by product states
\cdots|a\textnormal{{K}}\rangle|a\textnormal{{K}}\rangle|a\textnormal{{K}}\rangle\cdots
, indexed by coset representatives
a\in\textnormal{{G}}/\textnormal{{K}}
. There are
|\textnormal{{G}}/\textnormal{{K}}|\equiv|\textnormal{{G}}|/|\textnormal{{K}}|
ground states in total.

When treated as an edge of a quantum double model with no bulk excitations, the physical eigenstates of the edge theory are those for which
\overrightarrow{X}_{h}^{\textsf{(global)}}=1
for all
h\in\textnormal{{G}}
(
31
). The unique ground state in this sector is the equal superposition of all ground states,

|\text{gnd}_{\textnormal{{K}}}\rangle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\left|\textnormal{{G}}/\textnormal{{K}}\right|}}}\sum_{a\in\textnormal{{G}}/\textnormal{{K}}}\cdots|a\textnormal{{K}}\rangle|a\textnormal{{K}}\rangle|a\textnormal{{K}}\rangle\cdots\propto\sum_{\{g_{\mathsf{x}}\,,\,g_{\mathsf{x}}^{-1}g_{\mathsf{x}+1}\in\textnormal{{K}}\}}\cdots|g_{\mathsf{x}-1}\rangle|g_{\mathsf{x}}\rangle|g_{\mathsf{x}+1}\rangle\cdots~.

(37)

The presence of nontrivial bulk excitations can modify this ground state, with an anyonic excitation either mapping the state (
37
) to a ground state in this or another symmetry sector (for which it is said that the anyon
condenses
at the edge), or mapping the state to an excited state (the anyon becomes
confined
)
[
8
]
.
How the anyons condense depends on the anyon type as well as type of gapped edge
[
62
,
10
,
63
,
64
]
. While the general case is quite involved, the extreme cases
\textnormal{{K}}\in\{\langle 1\rangle,\textnormal{{G}}\}
serve as illuminating examples. The relevant excitation operators are

\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{1,\lambda}^{(2\mathsf{x})}=\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{(\mathsf{x})}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{g,\boldsymbol{1}}^{(2\mathsf{x})}=\cdots\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(\mathsf{x}-2)}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(\mathsf{x}-1)}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(\mathsf{x})}\,,

(38)

defined here using a convention we use later on. The first houses a charge excitation, while the second excites a flux at the edge. In the
\textnormal{{K}}=\langle 1\rangle
case, all charges condense on the edge:
\textsc{Tr}\{\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{1,\lambda}^{(\mathsf{x})}\}|\text{gnd}_{\langle 1\rangle}\rangle
remains in the ground-state subspace, while
\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{g,\boldsymbol{1}}^{(\mathsf{x})}|\text{gnd}_{\langle 1\rangle}\rangle
is a linear superposition of domain-wall excitations. In the
\textnormal{{K}}=\textnormal{{G}}
case, all fluxes condense: the separable state
|\text{gnd}_{\textnormal{{G}}}\rangle
is invariant under
\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{g,\boldsymbol{1}}
, while
\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{1,\lambda}
creates local excitations. In general,
\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{1,\lambda}
act as “order” operators creating local excitations, while
\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{g,\boldsymbol{1}}
act as “disorder” operators creating domain walls
[
113
,
114
,
115
]
.

Since the excitation operators (
38
) do not commute with the global symmetry
\overrightarrow{X}^{\textsf{(global)}}
, they can be interpreted as anyonic strings that straddle the bulk and edge. We will see them once more as special cases of our 1D flattened ribbon operators in the next section. They also modify the boundary conditions and symmetry sector of the model, which are explained in Appx.
A
.

3.6
Self-dual edge

Another notable instance of the flux ladder is the linear combination of
\textnormal{{K}}=\langle 1\rangle
and
\textnormal{{K}}=\textnormal{{G}}
gapped-edge Hamiltonians (
35
),

\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{G}}}^{\text{sd}}=\widehat{H}_{\langle 1\rangle\subseteq\textnormal{{G}}}^{\text{edge}}+\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{G}}\subseteq\textnormal{{G}}}^{\text{edge}}=-{\textstyle\frac{1}{|\textnormal{{G}}|}}\sum_{\mathsf{x}}\,\,\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{1}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+1\right)}+|\textnormal{{G}}\rangle\langle\textnormal{{G}}|^{(\mathsf{x})}~.

(39)

The two-body term becomes a projection onto the symmetric
subspace, comprised of the states
|g,g\rangle
. At the same time, the competing one-body
term, with coefficients
\text{f}_{g}=\frac{1}{|\textnormal{{G}}|}
, becomes a projection onto the state
|\textnormal{{G}}\rangle
(
36
) — an equal superposition of all
|g\rangle
at each site. The global symmetry of this case is the entire permutation group of
|\textnormal{{G}}|
objects, represented by tensor products of permutation matrices.

The Hamiltonian (
39
) represents a “self-dual” point in the flux ladder’s parameter space, as the two types of gapped edges that comprise it are related via Morita equivalence
[
10
,
116
]
. The Hamiltonian also corresponds to the self-dual point of the
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N=|\textnormal{{G}}|}
clock model
[
117
,
118
,
119
,
43
,
114
,
91
,
115
]
because it can equivalently be expressed in terms of Pauli
\widehat{X},\widehat{Z}
matrices associated with
|\textnormal{{G}}|
-dimensional qunits. We relate this point to a fermionizable point in the parameter space of the flux ladder’s continuum version in Sec.
6
.

4
Ribbon and Jordan-Wigner operators

A key feature of an anyonic string in the
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
surface code is that it violates plaquette and/or star constraints only at its ends, while commuting with all Hamiltonian terms supported on its body. In this way, the body can be moved around (as long as the ends are fixed) without changing the system’s energy. Their 1D analogues on the surface-code edge are tensor-product “strings” of
\widehat{X}
operators acting on the clock model (
9
), similarly carrying energy only at their ends. The symmetry
\widehat{X}^{\textsf{(global)}}
(
12
) is the special case of a string occupying the entire chain.

Quantum-double analogues of anyonic strings — ribbon operators
[
1
]
— maintain the key property of carrying energy only at their ends, but can no longer be expressed as a tensor product of single-site terms. Projecting them onto the effective bulk ground-state space yields
flattened ribbons
, one of the main results of this work that we present in Sec.
4.1
. In Sec.
4.2
, we superimpose flattened ribbons to obtain Jordan-Wigner (JW) operators
\overleftarrow{\varLambda}
associated with defects of quantum-double models. We simplify the form of the JW operators by introducing their “decoupled” versions
\overrightarrow{\varGamma}
in Sec.
4.3
, and use them to render the flux ladder (
27
).

4.1
Flattened ribbons

Figure 5:
(a) Sketch of a ribbon operator in the
bulk of a quantum double model
[
1
]
, which acts on sites
(red) of a ladder-like region of the lattice outlined by linked triangles
(blue). Here,
\widehat{\varPi}_{g}=|g\rangle\langle g|
. (b) A flattened ribbon (
43
), acting on a 1D chain and obtained by taking an ordinary ribbon and applying the bulk ground-state constraints from Sec.
3.4
.

Flattened ribbons are obtained by projecting ordinary ribbons onto the space of the effective flux-ladder edge theory. We omit such a derivation here for simplicity and instead elaborate on the flattened ribbons’ properties. We will need the formulas

\displaystyle\overleftarrow{X}_{k}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{h}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\right)}\overleftarrow{X}_{k}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}

\displaystyle=\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{kh}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\right)}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overleftarrow{X}_{k}^{\left(\mathsf{y}\right)}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{h}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\right)}\overleftarrow{X}_{k}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{y}\right)}=\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{hk^{-1}}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\right)}\,,

(40)

as well as the “multiplication” and “addition” rules for
\overleftarrow{\varPi}
,

\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{a}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\right)}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{b}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\right)}=\delta_{a,b}^{\textnormal{{G}}}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{a}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\right)}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\sum_{b\in\textnormal{{G}}}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{ab}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{z}\right)}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{b^{-1}c}^{\left(\mathsf{z},\mathsf{y}\right)}=\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{ac}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\right)}\,.

(41)

A flattened ribbon has support on a chain of sites from
\mathsf{x}
to
\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}
,
with its two
ends
being
\mathsf{x}
and
\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}
and the
body

consisting of all sites inbetween. A ribbon
\overleftarrow{F}
with the smallest
nontrivial body lies on three sites,

\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{[1,3]}=\sum_{a\in\textnormal{{G}}}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{h^{-1}a}^{\left(1,2\right)}\overleftarrow{X}_{a^{-1}ha}^{\left(2\right)}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{a^{-1}g}^{\left(2,3\right)}\,,

(42)

where
h,g\in\textnormal{{G}}
. A nontrivial property of a ribbon is that
it commutes with any
\overleftarrow{X}
and
\overleftarrow{\varPi}
supported on sites within its
body. The body of the above ribbon lies on site
2
, large enough
to support an
\overleftarrow{X}
. Using Eqs. (
40
)
and
\overleftarrow{X}_{k}\overleftarrow{X}_{g}=\overleftarrow{X}_{kgk^{-1}}\overleftarrow{X}_{k}
, conjugation of
\overleftarrow{F}

by
\overleftarrow{X}_{k}^{\left(2\right)}
is equivalent to letting
a\to ak

in the sum, which merely permutes its terms.

Longer ribbons follow a similar pattern of
\overleftarrow{X}
’s intertwined by

\overleftarrow{\varPi}
’s,

\boxed{\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{[\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}]}=\sum_{\{a_{\iota}\}\in\textnormal{{G}}^{\times(\mathsf{y}-1)}}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{h^{-1}a_{1}}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\overleftarrow{X}_{a_{1}^{-1}ha_{1}}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{a_{1}^{-1}h^{-1}a_{2}}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1,\mathsf{x}+2\right)}\overleftarrow{X}_{a_{2}^{-1}ha_{2}}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+2\right)}\cdots\overleftarrow{X}_{a_{\mathsf{y}-1}^{-1}ha_{\mathsf{y}-1}}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}-1\right)}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{a_{\mathsf{y}-1}^{-1}g}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}-1,\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}\right)}}\,.

(43)

Ribbons commute with
\overrightarrow{X}^{\textsf{(global)}}
(
31
) since both
\overleftarrow{X}
and
\overleftarrow{\varPi}
do so. For the
\textnormal{{G}}=\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
case,
sequential use of the addition formula (
41
)
collapses the intermediate
\widehat{\varPi}
’s and yields the string
\widehat{X}_{h}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\widehat{X}_{h}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+2\right)}\cdots\widehat{X}_{h}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}-1\right)}\widehat{\varPi}_{g}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}\right)}
.

Now we show that flattened ribbons commute with any
\overleftarrow{\varPi}
’s within their body.
Letting
\mathsf{x}=0
, consider permuting
\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{k}
on sites
1
and
2
through the ribbon. This projection interacts
only with three operators (
\overleftarrow{X}^{\left(1\right)}
,
\overleftarrow{\varPi}^{\left(1,2\right)}
,
and
\overleftarrow{X}^{\left(2\right)}
) in each term in the ribbon’s sum.
Permuting those operators through with the help of Eqs. (
40
)
yields

\left(\overleftarrow{X}_{a_{1}^{-1}ha_{1}}^{\left(1\right)}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{a_{1}^{-1}h^{-1}a_{2}}^{\left(1,2\right)}\overleftarrow{X}_{a_{2}^{-1}ha_{2}}^{\left(2\right)}\right)\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{k}^{\left(1,2\right)}=\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{a_{1}^{-1}ha_{1}ka_{2}^{-1}h^{-1}a_{2}}^{\left(1,2\right)}\left(\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}}^{\left(1,2\right)}\overleftarrow{X}_{a_{1}^{-1}ha_{1}}^{\left(1\right)}\overleftarrow{X}_{a_{2}^{-1}ha_{2}}^{\left(2\right)}\right).

(44)

Using the multiplication rule (
41
) for the
two
\overleftarrow{\varPi}
’s yields zero unless
a_{1}^{-1}ha_{1}ka_{2}^{-1}h^{-1}a_{2}=a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}
,
which simplifies to
k=a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}
. Thus, we can substitute

a_{1}^{-1}ha_{1}ka_{2}^{-1}h^{-1}a_{2}\to k
, completing the proof.

The above ribbons have a
\overleftarrow{\varPi}
on each end, and ribbons ending with

\overleftarrow{X}
are defined as

\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{\llbracket\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}]}=\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{[\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}]}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{[\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}\rrbracket}=\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{[\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}]}\overleftarrow{X}_{g^{-1}hg}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}\right)}\,.

(45)

In general, flattened ribbons can be recursively constructed from the minimal
ribbons

\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{\left[\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+1\right]}=\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{g}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{\llbracket\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}\rrbracket}=\overleftarrow{X}_{h}\delta_{hg,1}\,,

(46)

along with the “gluing” relation, for
\mathsf{x}\leq\mathsf{z}\leq\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}
,

\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{[\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}]}=\sum_{a\in\textnormal{{G}}}\overleftarrow{F}_{h,h^{-1}a}^{[\mathsf{x},\mathsf{z}]}\overleftarrow{F}_{a^{-1}ha,a^{-1}g}^{\llbracket\mathsf{z},\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}]}=\sum_{a\in\textnormal{{G}}}\overleftarrow{F}_{h,h^{-1}a}^{[\mathsf{x},\mathsf{z}\rrbracket}\overleftarrow{F}_{a^{-1}ha,a^{-1}g}^{[\mathsf{z},\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}]}\,.

(47)

Ribbons with nontrivial bodies (
\mathsf{y}\geq 2
) satisfy

\displaystyle\overleftarrow{F}_{h_{1},g_{1}}^{[\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}]}\overleftarrow{F}_{h_{2},g_{2}}^{[\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}]}

\displaystyle=\delta_{g_{2},g_{1}}^{\textnormal{{G}}}\overleftarrow{F}_{h_{2}h_{1},g_{1}}^{[\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}]}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{\dagger[\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}]}=\overleftarrow{F}_{h^{-1},g}^{[\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}]}\,.

(48)

They form a basis for all
\overrightarrow{X}^{\textsf{(global)}}
-symmetric operators, and the
flux ladder (
27
) can be expressed using the
minimal ribbons (
46
).

Flattened ribbons
\overleftarrow{F}^{\llbracket\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\rrbracket}
satisfy the same commutation relations with Hamiltonian operators at the ribbons’ ends as ordinary ribbons do with plaquette and star operators of the bulk quantum double {
[
62
]
, Eqs. (9-12)}, namely,

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\begin{aligned} \overleftarrow{X}_{k}^{(\mathsf{x})}\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{\llbracket\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\rrbracket}&=\overleftarrow{F}_{khk^{-1},kg}^{\llbracket\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\rrbracket}\overleftarrow{X}_{k}^{(\mathsf{x})}\\
\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{k}^{\left(\mathsf{x}-1,\mathsf{x}\right)}\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{\llbracket\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\rrbracket}&=\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{\llbracket\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\rrbracket}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{kh}^{\left(\mathsf{x}-1,\mathsf{x}\right)}\end{aligned}&&\begin{aligned} \overleftarrow{X}_{k}^{(\mathsf{y})}\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{\llbracket\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\rrbracket}&=\overleftarrow{F}_{h,gk^{-1}}^{\llbracket\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\rrbracket}\overleftarrow{X}_{k}^{(\mathsf{y})}\\
\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{k}^{\left(\mathsf{y},\mathsf{y}+1\right)}\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{\llbracket\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\rrbracket}&=\overleftarrow{F}_{h,g}^{\llbracket\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\rrbracket}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{g^{-1}h^{-1}gk}^{\left(\mathsf{y},\mathsf{y}+1\right)}\end{aligned}\end{array}\,.

(49)

This means that flattened ribbons carry pairs of anyonic excitations at their ends, forming a basis for the algebra of anyonic excitations that can be used to determine which bulk anyons condense on particular gapped edges.
A given ribbon can carry multiple anyons, but particular superpositions of ribbons carrying a desired anyon can be constructed via a basis change [
[
61
]
, Eq. (B66); see also
[
64
]
, Thm. 2.6]. Examples of ribbons carrying charge and flux anyons are shown in Table
2
. An alternative basis expressing explicit edge excitations can also be constructed {
[
63
]
, Sec. 2.3.3}. Flattened ribbons cannot change the bulk anyonic data, however, since they commute with the global symmetry
\overrightarrow{X}^{\textsf{(global)}}
(
31
). They thus represent strictly edge excitations that do no change the symmetry sector of the model.

Excitations on edge

Expression

Ribbon

charges
\lambda

{\displaystyle\phantom{\sum_{g\in\textnormal{{G}}}}{\displaystyle\text{Tr}\big[\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{\dagger(\mathsf{x})}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{(\mathsf{y})}\big]}\phantom{\sum_{g\in\textnormal{{G}}}}}

{\displaystyle\sum_{g\in\textnormal{{G}}}\text{Tr}\left[\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}\left(g\right)\right]\overleftarrow{F}_{1,g}^{[\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}]}}

fluxes
\text{cls}(g)

{\displaystyle\sum_{\{a_{\iota}\}\in\textnormal{{G}}}\overleftarrow{X}_{g}^{(\mathsf{x})}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{g^{-1}a_{1}}^{\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\overleftarrow{X}_{a_{1}^{-1}ga_{2}}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\cdots\overleftarrow{X}_{g}^{(\mathsf{y})}}

\lambda

{\displaystyle\text{Tr}\big[\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{1,\lambda}^{\left(2\mathsf{x}\right)}\big]}

{\displaystyle{\displaystyle\cdots\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(\mathsf{x}-2)}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(\mathsf{x}-1)}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(\mathsf{x})}}}
