Table 2:
Anyonic excitations on the edge of a quantum double can come from ribbon operators that are confined to the edge (“Excitations on edge” in the above table), or those that come from the bulk and touch the edge with only one end (“Excitations from bulk”). The former corresponds to flattened ribbons
\overleftarrow{F}
(
43
), which cause anyonic excitations at each of their two ends. Rows two and three of the above table contains examples of such ribbons. Examples of the latter, shown in the last two rows, can be represented by the decoupled Jordan-Wigner operators
\overrightarrow{\varGamma}
(
4.3
).

4.2
Jordan-Wigner operators

Having covered how to construct anyonic excitations out of flattened ribbons, here we convert our ribbons into those that touch the edge at only one end. This generalizes the Jordan-Wigner mapping to general groups, yielding a way to construct point defects between flux ladders in different phases.

Recall that
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
Jordan-Wigner (JW) operators
[
43
,
44
,
47
]
are
of the form
\widehat{X}\widehat{X}\cdots\widehat{Z}
,
consisting of a
tail
of
\widehat{X}
operators labeled by a group element
h
, and a
\widehat{Z}
-operator
head
labeled by an irrep
\lambda
. “Two-headed”
versions of such operators (beginning and ending with a
\widehat{Z}
operator) can be obtained by properly superimposing
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
ribbons,

\sum_{b\in\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}e^{i\frac{2\pi}{N}\lambda b}\overleftarrow{F}_{h,b}^{[\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}]}=\widehat{Z}_{\lambda}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\widehat{X}_{h}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\widehat{X}_{h}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+2\right)}\cdots\widehat{X}_{h}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}-1\right)}\widehat{Z}_{\lambda}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{y}\right)}\,.

(50)

These create an anyonic string carrying one charge and one flux excitation at each end.
In order to obtain a bona-fide JW string, we remove the effect of the leftmost
\widehat{Z}_{\lambda}^{\dagger}
by placing
it on a site far away from the chain.
For a chain with sites
\mathsf{x}=1
through
\mathsf{L}
and open boundary conditions,
the
\widehat{Z}_{\lambda}^{\dagger}
can be placed at a site symbolically denoted as

\mathsf{x}=0
.

Extending to the non-Abelian case via Eq. (
29
),
we introduce JW-operators

\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{g,\lambda}^{\left(2\mathsf{x}-1\right)}=\sum_{b\in\textnormal{{G}}}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}\left(b\right)\overleftarrow{F}_{g,b}^{[0,\mathsf{x}]}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{g,\lambda}^{\left(2\mathsf{x}\right)}=\sum_{b\in\textnormal{{G}}}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}\left(b\right)\overleftarrow{F}_{g,b}^{[0,\mathsf{x}\rrbracket}\,,

(51)

where the two types correspond to terminations
\cdots\widehat{X}_{g}^{\left(\mathsf{x}-1\right)}\widehat{Z}_{\lambda}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}

and
\cdots\widehat{X}_{g}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\widehat{Z}_{\lambda}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}
for
the
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
case, respectively.
Acting on both the group and
internal spaces, they satisfy

\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{g,\tau}^{\left(\mathsf{m}\right)}\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{h,\lambda}^{\left(\mathsf{n}\right)}=\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}\left(g\right).\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{g^{-1}hg,\lambda}^{\left(\mathsf{n}\right)}\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{g,\tau}^{\left(\mathsf{m}\right)}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{g,\lambda}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{n}\right)}.\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{g,\lambda}^{\left(\mathsf{n}\right)}=1_{\lambda}\otimes 1\,,

(52)

where
\mathsf{m}>\mathsf{n}
,
1_{\lambda}
is the identity on the internal irrep space
\lambda
[see Eq. (
14
)],
and
1
the identity on the group space. The
\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}(k)
matrix
generalizes the root-of-unity phase present in the
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
(parafermionic)
case, while the conjugation
g^{-1}hg
is a side-effect
of the non-Abelian case. Just like ribbons, single JW operators generally carry multiple anyonic excitations at their ends. A notable exception is
\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{1,\lambda}
, which carries pure charge excitations
\lambda
.

JW operators can also be
fused
via tensor products
[
109
]
in the irrep space, which reduce to ordinary multiplication in the abelian case. The tensor product of two irreps
\tau,\lambda
can be decomposed into a direct sum over some irreps,
\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\tau}\otimes\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}=\bigoplus_{\mu}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\mu}
. A given irrep can be present more than once in the sum. This yields the fusion rules

\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{h,\tau}^{\left(2\mathsf{x}-1\right)}\otimes\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{g,\lambda}^{\left(2\mathsf{x}-1\right)}=\bigoplus_{\mu}\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{gh,\mu}^{\left(2\mathsf{x}-1\right)}~.

(53)

JW operators do not commute with the global symmetry
\overrightarrow{X}^{\textsf{(global)}}
, which means they violate the bulk ground-state constraint (
31
) and can be used to change the bulk anyonic data. These operators thus correspond to flattened ribbons whose end at
\mathsf{x}=0
dips into and causes an excitation in the bulk. Treating the bulk as very far away,
we define
local
operators to be symmetric products of a few nearby operators. For example, a single
\overleftarrow{\varLambda}^{(\mathsf{n})}
is not local since it is coupled to the bulk site
\mathsf{x}=0
, while the product

\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{g,\lambda}^{\dagger\left(2\mathsf{x}\right)}.\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{g,\lambda}^{\left(2\mathsf{x}+1\right)}=\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}

is local for any
g,\lambda
. This product can be used to express the most general two-body term of the flux ladder (
27
).
Surprisingly, the other product of JW operators is
not
local. After using Eqs. (
45
) and
(
48
), the resulting combination remains supported on the bulk site,

\displaystyle\textsc{Tr}\big[\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{g,\lambda}^{\dagger\left(2\mathsf{x}-1\right)}.\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{g,\lambda}^{\left(2\mathsf{x}\right)}\big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{b,c\in\textnormal{{G}}}\textsc{Tr}\big[\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}(b^{-1}c)\big]\overleftarrow{F}_{g^{-1},b}^{[0,\mathsf{x}]}\overleftarrow{F}_{g,c}^{[0,\mathsf{x}]}\overleftarrow{X}_{c^{-1}gc}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}=d_{\lambda}\sum_{b\in\textnormal{{G}}}\overleftarrow{\varPi}_{b}^{(0,\mathsf{x})}\overleftarrow{X}_{b^{-1}gb}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\,.

Since the above is not equal to a
\overleftarrow{X}^{(\mathsf{x})}_{g}
, a workaround is required in order to render the single-body term in the flux ladder with products of two JW operators.

4.3
Decoupled Jordan-Wigner operators

One way to circumvent the above obstruction and construct JW operators that can be used to express the flux ladder is to fix the outside
\mathsf{x}=0
site to be in the identity state
|g=1\rangle
. This collapses the flattened ribbons’ alternating products of
\overleftarrow{\varPi}
and
\overleftarrow{X}
into a tensor product of
\overrightarrow{X}
using

\sum_{a\in\textnormal{{G}}}|a\rangle\langle a|\overleftarrow{X}_{a^{-1}g^{-1}a}=\overrightarrow{X}_{g}~.

(56)

We define “decoupled” versions of the JW operators, which (analogous to the abelian case) consist of a product of
\overrightarrow{X}
’s for a tail and a

–

\widehat{Z}

head,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{g,\lambda}^{\left(2\mathsf{x}-1\right)}\equiv\langle 1|^{\left(0\right)}\overleftarrow{\varLambda}_{g^{-1},\lambda}^{\left(2\mathsf{x}-1\right)}\,|1\rangle^{\left(0\right)}=\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{\left(1\right)}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{\left(2\right)}\cdots\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{\left(\mathsf{x}-1\right)}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}~,

and similarly,
\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{g,\lambda}^{\left(2\mathsf{x}\right)}\equiv\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{\left(1\right)}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{\left(2\right)}\cdots\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}
. These are precisely combinations of the “order-disorder” bulk excitation operators encountered in our analysis of gapped edges [see Eq. (
38
) and Table
2
]. Equation (
4.3
) shows how such simplified bulk excitations can be obtained from superpositions of flattened ribbons that straddle the bulk and edge of the system.

Simple nearest-neighbor combinations of decoupled JW operators readily express a subset of all possible flux ladder Hamiltonians. One combination renders a single
\overrightarrow{X}
,
while the other product renders the two-body
\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}^{\dagger}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}
term. Combining these yields

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{G}}}^{\text{JW}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\mathsf{x}}\,\,\sum_{\lambda\in\text{irr}(\textnormal{{G}})}\textsc{Tr}\big[\text{C}_{\lambda}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\big]+\sum_{g\in\textnormal{{G}}}\text{f}_{g}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\mathsf{x}}\,\,\sum_{\lambda\in\text{irr}(\textnormal{{G}})}\textsc{Tr}\big[\text{C}_{\lambda}.\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{a,\lambda}^{\dagger\left(2\mathsf{x}\right)}.\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{a,\lambda}^{\left(2\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\big]+\sum_{g\in\textnormal{{G}}}\text{f}_{g}{\textstyle\frac{1}{d_{\sigma}}}\textsc{Tr}\big[\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{g,\sigma}^{\dagger\left(2\mathsf{x}-1\right)}.\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{g,\sigma}^{\left(2\mathsf{x}\right)}\big]~,

(59e)

valid for any group element
a
and irrep
\sigma
. The difference between this Hamiltonian and the general flux ladder (
27a
) is the direction of the arrow above the one-body terms. In order for the sum of such terms to commute with
\overrightarrow{X}_{k}^{\textsf{(global)}}
, the coefficients have to be invariant under conjugation,
\text{f}_{k^{-1}gk}=\text{f}_{g}
. That way, the sum is invariant after a change of variables,

\overrightarrow{X}_{k}\left(\sum_{g\in\textnormal{{G}}}\text{f}_{g}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}\right)\overrightarrow{X}_{k}=\sum_{g\in\textnormal{{G}}}\text{f}_{g}\overrightarrow{X}_{kgk^{-1}}=\sum_{g\in\textnormal{{G}}}\text{f}_{k^{-1}gk}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}=\sum_{g\in\textnormal{{G}}}\text{f}_{g}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}\,.

(60)

The most general form of invariant coefficients is
\text{f}_{g}=\sum_{\lambda\in\text{irr}(\textnormal{{G}})}\text{f}_{\lambda}\textsc{Tr}[\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}(g)]
, with coefficients
\{\text{f}_{\lambda}\}
defined such that the flux ladder is Hermitian. This Hamiltonian is, perhaps coincidentally, identical to that considered in Ref.
[
71
]
, and our decoupled JW construction offers a quantum-double inspired alternative to their dyonic-mode construction.

General
\overleftarrow{X}_{g}
operators can be expressed using products of JW operators with mismatched “heads”. Using a trick similar to Eq. (
56
) to express
\overleftarrow{X}
in terms of
\overrightarrow{X}
and writing
|h\rangle\langle h|
as a sum of
Z
-type operators using orthogonality relations (
21
) yields

\displaystyle\overleftarrow{X}_{g}^{(\mathsf{x})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in\textnormal{{G}}}\overrightarrow{X}_{h^{-1}g^{-1}h}^{(\mathsf{x})}\sum_{\lambda\in\text{irr}(\textnormal{{G}})}{\textstyle\frac{d_{\lambda}}{\left|\textnormal{{G}}\right|}}\textsc{Tr}\left[\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}\left(h\right).\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{(\mathsf{x})}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in\textnormal{{G}}}\sum_{\lambda\in\text{irr}(\textnormal{{G}})}{\textstyle\frac{d_{\lambda}}{\left|\textnormal{{G}}\right|}}\textsc{Tr}\big[\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}\left(h\right).\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{h^{-1}g^{-1}h,\boldsymbol{1}}^{\dagger\left(2\mathsf{x}-1\right)}.\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{h^{-1}g^{-1}h,\lambda}^{\left(2\mathsf{x}\right)}\big]~.

While this means the general flux ladder can be expressed in terms of decoupled JW bilinears, we stick with the simpler case of Eq. (
59
) for the example below.

Dihedral case

Using decoupled JW operators (
4.3
) leaves freedom to choose the types “heads”
\sigma
and “tails”
a
for constructing the respective one- and two- body terms in
H^{\text{JW}}_{\textnormal{{G}}}
(
59
). Similar to the
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
case, we do not need all possible combinations to express the Hamiltonian for
\textnormal{{G}}=\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
. We can develop a “generating set” of
\{g,\lambda\}
, appealing to the correspondence between irreps and conjugacy classes
[
109
]
.
As an example for odd
N
, consider, respectively, the parafermion-like and Majorana-like operators

\overrightarrow{\eta}_{\tau}\equiv\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{g=r^{\tau},\lambda=\boldsymbol{2}_{\tau}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overrightarrow{\gamma}\equiv\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{g=p,\lambda=\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}}~.

(62)

There are
(N+1)/2

parafermion-like operators since that is the number of 2D irreps labeled by
\tau\in\{1,2,\cdots,\left(N-1\right)/2\}
. Each irrep is paired with the
\tau^{\text{th}}
power of the rotation
r
. The remaining one-dimensional irrep is paired with the reflection
p
to make the Majorana operator. The even
N
case presents two more Majorana-like pairs labeled by
\{r^{N/2},\boldsymbol{1}_{+}\}

and
\{pr,\boldsymbol{1}_{-}\}
. Since their irreps are one-dimensional, all Majorana-like operators act only on the group space and square to the identity. Applying Eq. (
52
), the two respective sets of operators mimic parafermionic and Majorana commutation relations; for
\mathsf{n}<\mathsf{m}
,

\overrightarrow{\eta}_{\sigma}^{\left(\mathsf{m}\right)}\overrightarrow{\eta}_{\tau}^{\left(\mathsf{n}\right)}=\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\boldsymbol{2}_{\tau}}\left(r\right).\overrightarrow{\eta}_{\tau}^{\left(\mathsf{n}\right)}\overrightarrow{\eta}_{\sigma}^{\left(\mathsf{m}\right)}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overrightarrow{\gamma}^{\left(\mathsf{m}\right)}\overrightarrow{\gamma}^{\left(\mathsf{n}\right)}=-\overrightarrow{\gamma}^{\left(\mathsf{n}\right)}\overrightarrow{\gamma}^{\left(\mathsf{m}\right)}~,

(63)

where
\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\boldsymbol{2}_{\tau}}(r)
(
16
) is a diagonal matrix with root-of-unity entries. However,
\overrightarrow{\eta}
does not commute with
\overrightarrow{\gamma}
, and their various combinations render the terms in
\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}^{\text{JW}}
(
59
).

Armed with this construction, we can postulate the existence of JW operators that commute with the Hamiltonian and that are stable to small perturbations. Such operators, if they exist, would correspond to extensions of
\textnormal{{Z}}_{2}
Majorana and
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
parafermion zero modes to
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
. Such extensions could lead to novel topologically protected quantum computation schemes based on non-Abelian groups.

Consider the limiting case
\text{f}=0
of the flux ladder. The operators
\overrightarrow{\gamma}^{(1)}
,
\overrightarrow{\gamma}^{(2\mathsf{L})}
,
\overrightarrow{\eta}^{(1)}
, and
\overrightarrow{\eta}^{(2\mathsf{L})}
are not present in and commute with
\widehat{H}^{\text{JW}}_{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}
, corresponding to trivial examples of zero modes. Robustness under
\text{f}\ll 1
perturbations would make them nontrivial, but such stability is not guaranteed already in the abelian
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N>2}
case. There is numerical evidence that parafermion zero modes are stable around special points of the clock model, where the spectrum
of the two-body term happens to be symmetric around zero
[
47
,
87
,
88
]
.
In Appx.
B
, we identify a point in the
\textnormal{{D}}_{3}

flux ladder’s parameter space with that feature, pointing to a special point at which these modes might be stable. Stability of such modes could also be related to stability of the dyonic modes of Ref.
[
71
]
, and it may be possible to construct weaker versions of zero modes that are robust and that commute with a projected version of the flux ladder.

We conclude this section with a summary. We construct strictly 1D (i.e., “flattened”) versions of quantum-double ribbon operators that carry anyonic excitations on the flux ladder — an effective theory for the quantum-double edge (see Fig.
5
). We then construct superpositions of flattened ribbons that extend the Jordan-Wigner mapping to non-Abelian groups. In the quantum double framework, this provides a way to construct anyonic zero mode operators carrying anyons that condense on a point defect between two different boundaries, but not the boundaries themselves. Assuming the flux ladder is treated as an edge theory of a quantum double topological phase, some JW operators may bind generalized zero modes that are robust to noise and that generalize Majorana and parafermion zero modes. We leave determination of such robustness to future work.

5
Continuum description of the flux ladder

The rest of the paper is devoted to realizing instances of the general flux ladder with electrons near the Fermi level. The motivation is to foster realization of quantum-double non-Abelian defects beyond Majorana and parafermion zero modes in real materials. The procedure consists of two parts, (1) developing a continuum version of the flux ladder model, and (2) recasting an instance of the resulting continuum field theory in terms of fermionic operators. We develop the part (1) here, and part (2) in the next section.

The general flux ladder acts on sites that are valued in a finite group
G
, an inherently discrete object. In order to “smooth out” this group space, we
embed
the flux ladder into another one acting on sites that are valued in a connected Lie group
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\supset\textnormal{{G}}
. Such an embedding works equally well for the group-valued “position” and irrep-valued “momentum” spaces of the sites. Group elements
g\in\textnormal{{G}}
, labeling
X
-type operators, naturally correspond to some chosen elements of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
. Any irrep
\lambda\in\text{irr}(\textnormal{{G}})
, labeling
Z
-type operators, can be obtained from an irrep
\ell
of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
whenever the corresponding irrep matrix
U_{\ell}
contains (i.e.,
branches to
) a copy of
\lambda
when evaluated at elements of
G
. Operators we need for our procedure are summarized in Table
3
for the known
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{{U}}(1)
embedding and a dihedral version introduced here.

This
\textnormal{{G}}\hookrightarrow\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
embedding yields expressions of all terms of the original flux ladder in terms of exponentials of the Lie algebra of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
, allowing us to perform smooth manipulations that were not possible with the original discrete model. The field theory — a generalized
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
principal chiral model — is then obtained by
linearization

[
19
]
, i.e., expansion of all exponentials in the continuum limit.

\textnormal{{G}}\hookrightarrow\boldsymbol{\Gamma}

\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{{U}}(1)

\textnormal{{D}}_{N}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{{SO}}(3)

Z
-type operators

\phantom{\phantom{{\displaystyle\sum_{g}}}}\widehat{Z}_{\lambda}\hookrightarrow e^{i\lambda\widehat{\phi}}\phantom{\phantom{{\displaystyle\sum_{g}}}}

\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}\hookrightarrow\widehat{U}_{\ell_{\lambda}}=e^{-i\widehat{\phi}^{\texttt{a}}L_{\ell_{\lambda}}^{\texttt{a}}}

X
-type operators

\widehat{X}_{h}\hookrightarrow e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{N}h\widehat{L}}

\overrightarrow{X}_{g}\hookrightarrow\overrightarrow{X}_{R=g}=e^{-i\phi_{R}^{\texttt{a}}\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}}

\overleftarrow{X}_{g}\hookrightarrow\overleftarrow{X}_{R=g}=e^{-i\phi_{R}^{\texttt{a}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}}

\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{G}}}
potential

\cos(N\widehat{\phi})

\phantom{{\displaystyle\sum_{g}^{G}}}\textsc{Tr}\big[\varPi_{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}.\widehat{U}_{\ell=N}\big]\phantom{{\displaystyle\sum_{g}^{G}}}

\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}/\textnormal{{G}}}
potential

\cos\big(\frac{2\pi}{N}\widehat{L}\big)

{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\cos\big({\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{z}}\big)+\cos\big(\pi\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{x}}\big)

Table 3:

A continuum description of the flux ladder model for a discrete group
G
can be obtained by embedding each
G
-valued site into a site valued in a connected Lie group
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\supset\textnormal{{G}}
. An often-used version of this procedure embeds a
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
-valued qunit into a
\textnormal{{U}}(1)
rotor, and we list the relevant operators of that construction in the second column. The
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
-potential is minimized at the rotor’s
N
equidistant angular positions forming the
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
qunit, while the
\textnormal{{U}}(1)/\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
-potential is minimized at angular momenta that are multiples of
N
. In Secs.
5
and
6
, we develop a non-Abelian generalization of this procedure. The corresponding operators for
G
being the dihedral group and
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
being the group of proper 3D rotations are listed in the third column.

5.1
Clock model to free boson

A conventional heuristic to construct a field theory from a lattice model is to identify the relevant fields and write down the simplest terms that respect the lattice model’s symmetry. For the clock model (
9
), the relevant field is a
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
-valued order parameter distinguishing between these the
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
symmetry-breaking phase in the
\text{f}\to 0
limit and the disordered phase in the
\text{C}\to 0
limit.
A minimal field theory describing said order parameter is that of a free boson, supplemented by the leading-order
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
-symmetric cosine potential (
[
89
]
, Sec. 5.5;
[
90
]
, pg. 186;
[
91
]
). Defining a
\textnormal{{U}}(1)
-valued field
e^{i\widehat{\phi}(\mathsf{x})}
with corresponding current
\partial\widehat{\phi}(\mathsf{x})=-ie^{-i\widehat{\phi}(\mathsf{x})}\partial_{\mathsf{x}}e^{i\widehat{\phi}(\mathsf{x})}
and conjugate angular momentum
\widehat{L}(\mathsf{x})
, the Hamiltonian is

\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}^{\text{edge}}\to\int\text{d}\mathsf{x}\,\,\text{C}\,\partial\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\partial\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)+\text{f}\,\widehat{L}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\widehat{L}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)-\text{M}\cos N\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\,.

(64)

The parameters
\text{C},\text{f},\text{M}
are real, and the field is pinned to values in
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
in the “semiclassical” limit
\text{M}\to\infty
. This limit induces a variant of the Higgs mechanism (e.g.,
[
108
,
120
]
or
[
121
]
, Sec. IV.A), under which the local
\textnormal{{U}}(1)
Hilbert space “breaks” into a
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
subspace. A further discretization of the spatial index
\mathsf{x}
recovers the clock-model Hamiltonian
[
14
]
.

The
reverse
of the above local symmetry-breaking procedure is effectively an embedding of each
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
-valued qunit of the clock model into the Hilbert space
\{|\phi\rangle,\phi\in[0,2\pi)=\textnormal{{U}}(1)\}
of a planar rotor, with the potential

\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}=\cos(N\widehat{\phi})

(65)

remembering the labels of the original qunit. In other words, one can think of the clock model (
9
) as a rotor
XY
-model with a strong pinning potential. A subsequent continuum limit and expansion of all operators maps the clock model to a pinned-boson field theory.

5.2
Embedding non-Abelian group spaces

The
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{{U}}(1)
local Hilbert space embedding and subsequent continuum expansion can be extended to the flux ladder using facts about representations of groups
[
109
]
and Lie algebras
[
122
]
. We describe how to embed the local
G
-labeled Hilbert space into that of a continuous group
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
that contains
G
. We restrict
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
to be compact and connected so that there is a discrete set of irreps and so that every group element can be expressed using the Lie algebra of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
. Such a
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
exists
1
1

1

Embedding
G
into a compact connected Lie group
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
can be done,
e.g., via a permutation group
\textnormal{{S}}_{M}
. By Cayley’s theorem, there
exists an
M
such that
\textnormal{{S}}_{M}
contains
G
. Matrices forming
the permutation representation of
\textnormal{{S}}_{M}
form a subgroup of
\textnormal{{O}}(M)
.
Such matrices have determinant
\pm 1
, and one can increase their
dimension by one and pad the new diagonal entry with
\pm 1
such
that the expanded matrices have determinant one. These expanded matrices
then form a subgroup of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}=\textnormal{{SO}}(M+1)
. For specific groups, one
should consider a more efficient embedding; e.g., the quaternions
can be embedded directly into
\textnormal{{SU}}(2)
.

for any
G
,
and here we further restrict to two particular families for simplicity — the special orthogonal [
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}=\textnormal{{SO}}(M)
for some
M
] and special unitary [
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}=\textnormal{{SU}}(M)
] groups. The general
\textnormal{{G}}\hookrightarrow\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
procedure is sketched below. Those wishing for more explicitness may enjoy a parallel description of the
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
example in Appx.
C
(see also Table
3
).

The
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
group space can be defined by the group basis
|R\rangle
with
R\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}

[
49
]
, and the difference from the finite group case is that the canonical basis states are not normalizable:
\langle R|S\rangle=\delta_{RS}^{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
, with the Dirac-like
\delta
being infinite when
R=S
and zero otherwise. This space admits
X
-type operators
\{\overleftarrow{X}_{R},\overrightarrow{X}_{R}\}
similar to those of
G
. Embedding the latter operators into the space of the former merely corresponds to picking a particular subgroup
\textnormal{{G}}\subset\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
, where for any
g\in\textnormal{{G}}
, there is a corresponding
R(g)\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
. We will abuse notation and use
R
to refer to elements of
G
from now on.

The larger group space also comes with group-valued
Z
-type operators

\widehat{U}_{\ell}=\int_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\text{d}R\,U_{\ell}(R)\,\left|R\right\rangle\left\langle R\right|\,,

(66)

where
\ell
labels irreps of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
,
U_{\ell}(R)
is an
\ell
-irrep matrix evaluated at
R
, and
\text{d}R
is the Haar measure on
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
satisfying
\int\text{d}R\equiv|\boldsymbol{\Gamma}|
. Naturally,
\{\overrightarrow{X},\overleftarrow{X},\widehat{U}\}
satisfy the Weyl-type relations (
15
)
with
\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}\to\widehat{U}
.

When restricted to values in
G
, the
U_{\ell}
matrices form a representation of
G
, which can be decomposed (i.e., block-diagonalized) into irreps
\lambda
of
G
as

U_{\ell}(R)=\bigoplus_{\lambda,\,\ell\downarrow\lambda}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}(R)

(67)

for any
R\in\textnormal{{G}}
. The irrep
\ell
is said to
branch
into a particular
\lambda
,
\ell\downarrow\lambda
, when
\lambda
is present in the above decomposition. Conversely,
\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}
operators can be embedded into any
\widehat{U}_{\ell}
,

\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}\hookrightarrow\widehat{U}_{\ell}~,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{ whenever }\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\ell\downarrow\lambda~.

(68)

There can be infinitely many
\ell
which branch into a given
\lambda
, making the embedding procedure not unique. For simplicity, one can pick an
\ell
with smallest dimension.

A given
U_{\ell}
can branch to more than one
\lambda
, and projections onto the
G
-irreps can be used to select the desired operator in the decomposition. The projection operator

\varPi_{\lambda}^{\textnormal{{G}}}={\textstyle\frac{d_{\lambda}}{|\textnormal{{G}}|}}\sum_{R\in\textnormal{{G}}}\textsc{Tr}\left[\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}\left(R^{-1}\right)\right]\,U_{\ell}\left(R\right)

(69)

acts on the
\ell
-irrep space, projecting onto all copies of irrep
\lambda
of the finite group. For example, when
\ell
branches to one copy,

\varPi_{\lambda}^{\textnormal{{G}}}.U_{\ell}(R)=\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}(R)\oplus 0~,

(70)

selecting the corresponding

–

Z

-operator. The nonnegative integer
\textsc{Tr}[\varPi_{\lambda}^{\textnormal{{G}}}]/d_{\lambda}
counts the number of copies, where
d_{\lambda}
is the dimension of the irrep. When there are multiple copies, we can substitute
\varPi_{\lambda}
with a projection onto the copy we desire.

Projections can be used to construct a minimal pinning potential,

\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{G}}}={\textstyle{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}}\textsc{Tr}[\varPi_{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\textnormal{{G}}}.\widehat{U}_{\underline{\ell}}]+\text{h.c.}~,

(71)

where
\varPi
projects onto a particular copy of the trivial irrep
\lambda=\boldsymbol{1}
of
G
. When
\textsc{Tr}[\varPi_{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\textnormal{{G}}}.\widehat{U}_{\underline{\ell}}]
is evaluated at
R\in\textnormal{{G}}
, we have

\textsc{Tr}[\varPi_{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\textnormal{{G}}}.U_{\underline{\ell}}(R)]\left|R\right\rangle=\textsc{Tr}[\varPi_{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\textnormal{{G}}}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\boldsymbol{1}}(R)]\left|R\right\rangle=\textsc{Tr}[\varPi_{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\textnormal{{G}}}]\left|R\right\rangle=\left|R\right\rangle~.

(72)

Since
|\textsc{Tr}[\varPi.U]|\leq\textsc{Tr}[\varPi]
for any unitary
U
, the potential is maximized at all elements of
G
. In order for the potential to be maximized
exclusively
at those elements, we require that
\underline{\ell}
branches into the trivial irrep selected by
\varPi
without going through the trivial irrep of any intermediate group
\textnormal{{G}}^{\prime}\supset\textnormal{{G}}
. In other words, if there exists a
\textnormal{{G}}^{\prime}\supset\textnormal{{G}}
and
\tau\in\text{irr}(\textnormal{{G}}^{\prime})
such that
\underline{\ell}\downarrow\tau\downarrow\boldsymbol{1}
, then
\underline{\ell}
and
\varPi
have to be such that
\tau\neq\boldsymbol{1}
. Without this requirement, the potential would also be maximized at elements of
\textnormal{{G}}^{\prime}
. For example, in the case
\textnormal{{U}}(1)\supset\textnormal{{Z}}_{2N}\supset\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
, the potential
\cos(2N\widehat{\phi})
, corresponding to the
\textnormal{{U}}(1)
-irrep
\underline{\ell}=2N
, is maximized not only at points in
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
, but also at those in
\textnormal{{Z}}_{2N}
.

To express
\widehat{V}^{\text{sharp}}_{\textnormal{{G}}}
— the explicit projection onto the group subspace — in
terms of
\widehat{U}
-operators, we need
all

\ell
for which

U_{\ell}
admits at least one trivial irrep of
G
. Compact groups
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
admit an analogue of the finite-group orthogonality relations (
21
), with the Kronecker-
\delta
replaced by a Dirac-
\delta
. Using those yields the projection

\displaystyle\widehat{V}^{\text{sharp}}_{\textnormal{{G}}}=\sum_{R\in\textnormal{{G}}}\left|R\right\rangle\left\langle R\right|=\sum_{R\in\textnormal{{G}}}\sum_{\ell\in\text{irr}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})}{\textstyle\frac{d_{\ell}}{|\boldsymbol{\Gamma}|}}\textsc{Tr}\left[U_{\ell}(R^{-1}).\widehat{U}_{\ell}\right]=\sum_{\ell\in\text{irr}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})}{\textstyle\frac{d_{\ell}}{|\boldsymbol{\Gamma}|/|\textnormal{{G}}|}}\textsc{Tr}\big[\varPi_{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\textnormal{{G}}}.\widehat{U}_{\ell}\big]\,,

(73)

where
d_{\ell}
is the dimension of irrep
\ell
. For the abelian
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{{U}}(1)
embedding,

\widehat{V}^{\text{sharp}}_{\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}=\sum_{h\in\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}\left|\phi={\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}}h\right\rangle\left\langle\phi={\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}}h\right|=\sum_{h\in\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}{\textstyle\frac{1}{2\pi}}\sum_{\ell\in\textnormal{{Z}}}e^{i\left(\widehat{\phi}-\frac{2\pi}{N}\ell h\right)}={\textstyle\frac{1}{2\pi/N}}\sum_{\ell\in\textnormal{{Z}}}e^{iN\ell\widehat{\phi}}\,.

(74)

Dihedral flux ladder

\text{C}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}

\text{f}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}

General edge (
27
)

{\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in\text{irr}(\textnormal{{D}}_{N})}{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\textsc{Tr}\big[L_{\ell_{\lambda}}^{\texttt{a}}.\text{C}_{\ell_{\lambda}}^{\text{edge}}.L_{\ell_{\lambda}}^{\texttt{b}}\big]}

{\displaystyle\sum_{g\in\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\text{f}_{g}^{\text{edge}}\phi_{g}^{\texttt{a}}\phi_{g}^{\texttt{b}}}

Gapped edge (
\textnormal{{K}}\subseteq\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
) (
33
)

{\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in\text{irr}(\textnormal{{D}}_{N})}{\textstyle\frac{d_{\lambda}}{4N/|\textnormal{{K}}|}}\textsc{Tr}\big[L_{\ell_{\lambda}}^{\texttt{a}}.\varPi_{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\textnormal{{K}}}.L_{\ell_{\lambda}}^{\texttt{b}}\big]}

{\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\textnormal{{K}}}{\textstyle\frac{1}{2\left|\textnormal{{K}}\right|}}\phi_{k}^{\texttt{a}}\phi_{k}^{\texttt{b}}}

Self-dual edge (fermionizable) (
39
)

\big({\textstyle\frac{\kappa}{8\pi}}\big)^{2}\delta_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}

\delta_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}

Table 4:
Parameter matrices for the quadratic terms of
the
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}=\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
principal chiral model (
80
), worked out in Appx.
C
. The
General edge
case is a continuum version of a general
\textnormal{{G}}=\textnormal{{D}}_{N}

flux ladder with parameters
\{\text{C}^{\text{edge}},\text{f}^{\text{edge}}\}
. The
Gapped edge
case
is a continuum version of the gapped-edge lattice models from Sec.
3.5
. The
Self-dual edge
case is a specific case expressible
in terms of fermions via addition of a WZNW term at level
\kappa
(see Sec.
6
).

5.3
Flux ladder to principal chiral model

To complete the mapping to a field theory, we express operators
\{\overrightarrow{X},\overleftarrow{X},\widehat{U}\}
as exponentials of the Lie algebra of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
and expand while taking the continuum limit. The
X
-type operators are expressed in terms of corresponding
momentum currents

\overrightarrow{L},\overleftarrow{L}
, e.g.,

\overleftarrow{X}_{R}^{(\mathsf{x})}=e^{-i\phi_{R}^{\texttt{a}}(\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}})^{(\mathsf{x})}}\approx 1-i\phi_{R}^{\texttt{a}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}(\mathsf{x})+\cdots~,

(75)

where
\phi_{R}^{\texttt{a}}
are the coordinates of
R
. A similar expansion can be done for
\overrightarrow{X}
. The
typewriter
-font letters index the generators of the Lie algebra of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
, and all repeated indices are summed.
The currents satisfy the commutation relations

\big[\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}},\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{b}}\big]=if_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{c}}\,;\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\big[\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}},\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{b}}\big]=if_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{c}}\,;\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\big[\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}},\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{b}}\big]=0~,

(76)

where
f_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}
are the algebra’s structure constants.

The group-valued fields
\widehat{U}_{\ell}
are expressed as exponentials of irreps of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
’s (compact) Lie algebra, whose generators
L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}}
satisfy

[L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}},L_{\ell}^{\texttt{b}}]=if_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}L_{\ell}^{\texttt{c}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textsc{Tr}\big[L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}}.L_{\ell}^{\texttt{b}}\big]=\delta_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}c_{\ell}~.

(77)

The last equation indicates that we have picked generators that are orthogonal under
the trace, with the normalization
c_{\ell}
known as
the Dynkin index. The two-body
ZZ
-type operator can then be embedded into a corresponding product of
\widehat{U}_{\ell}
’s and expanded as

\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}^{\dagger(\mathsf{x})}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}^{(\mathsf{x}+1)}\hookrightarrow\widehat{U}^{\dagger(\mathsf{x})}.\widehat{U}^{(\mathsf{x}+1)}\approx 1+\widehat{U}^{\dagger}.\partial\widehat{U}(\mathsf{x})+\cdots~,

(78)

where
\partial\equiv\partial_{\mathsf{x}}
. The expansion is expressed in terms of the
position current

\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}_{\ell}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)=-i\widehat{U}_{\ell}^{\dagger}.\partial\widehat{U}_{\ell}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)=\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{a}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{a}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)={\textstyle\frac{1}{c_{\ell}}}\textsc{Tr}\big[\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}_{\ell}\left(\mathsf{x}\right).L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}}\big]\,.

(79)

This current acts on both the internal and
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
spaces, but
its components
\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{a}}
act only on the latter. Note that the orthogonality of the generators and division by the Dynkin index has removed the dependence of the components on the particular irrep
\ell
.

The above expansions are performed under the assumption that the terms vary over space
smoothly and slowly. The values of the position current are assumed to change infinitesimally as
\mathsf{x}
is varied. To expand the momentum current, we assume that the Hamiltonian can only impart an infinitesimal change in the group-valued position at any
\mathsf{x}
. Combined with a pinning potential, the quadratic terms yield a generalized principal chiral model associated with
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
,

\boxed{\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{G}}}^{\text{edge}}\to\int\text{d}\mathsf{x}\,\,\text{C}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\,\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{a}}\!\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{b}}\!\left(\mathsf{x}\right)+\text{f}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}\!\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{b}}\!\left(\mathsf{x}\right)-{\textstyle{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}}\text{M}\big(\textsc{Tr}\big[\varPi_{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\textnormal{{G}}}.\widehat{U}_{\underline{\ell}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\big]+\text{h.c.}\big)}\,.

(80)

In the
\text{M}\to\infty
limit, the pinning potential (
71
) pins the
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
-valued Hilbert space to the
G
-valued subspace at each spatial point
\mathsf{x}
.

The quadratic terms in the above field theory are symmetric under

\overrightarrow{X}_{R}^{\textsf{(global)}}\equiv e^{-i\phi_{R}^{\texttt{a}}(\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}})^{\textsf{(global)}}}\,\,\,,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{generated by}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overrightarrow{L}^{\textsf{(global)}}\equiv\int\text{d}\mathsf{x}\,\overrightarrow{L}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)

(81)

for any
R\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
and any values of the parameter matrices
C
and
f
. The pinning potential is only invariant
under
R\in\textnormal{{G}}
, so the full theory has only
G
-symmetry. Bulk anyonic data, associated with treating the flux ladder as an edge theory for a
G
quantum double, also carry over to the continuum. The bulk ground-state subspace is fixed by
\overrightarrow{X}_{R}^{\textsf{(global)}}=1
for
all
R\in\textnormal{{G}}
(
31
); more general bulk constraints are discussed in Appx.
A
.

The other global operations
\overleftarrow{X}_{R}^{\textsf{(global)}}
are not guaranteed to be symmetries of the theory. Both currents transform under these operations in the
adjoint representation
,

\overleftarrow{X}_{R}^{\dagger\textsf{(global)}}\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{a}}\overleftarrow{X}_{R}^{\textsf{(global)}}=R_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{b}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overleftarrow{X}_{R}^{\dagger\textsf{(global)}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}\overleftarrow{X}_{R}^{\textsf{(global)}}=R_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{b}}\,,

(82)

which quantifies how the group rotates elements of its own Lie algebra. The
R_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}
is a real-valued matrix element of an orthogonal rotation, defined as

R_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}={\textstyle\frac{1}{c_{\ell}}}\textsc{Tr}\left[U_{\ell}^{\dagger}.L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}}.U_{\ell}.L_{\ell}^{\texttt{b}}\right].

(83)

The definition is independent of which irreps
\ell
one picks, as long as it is nontrivial.

Besides yielding a heuristic field theory, the mapping can be made explicit, with parameters of the flux
ladder directly absorbed into the matrices
\text{C}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}
and
\text{f}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}
. We have performed the explicit procedure for the case
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
in Appx.
C
, and the resulting parameters are shown in Table
4
for the three cases studied in Sec.
3
. While we should not assign too much importance to potentially numerological values of
\{\text{C}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}},\text{f}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\}
, the procedure nevertheless helps us identify general regions in the large parameter space of the field theory that may be related to particular edges of quantum double models.

6
Quantum wire constructions

In the previous section, we developed a continuum version of the flux ladder, an effective lattice model for the quantum-double edge. In this section, we recast a particular instance of the version in terms of fermionic fields. This result makes first contact between the quantum double edge and quantum wires, with the long-term goal of realizing continuum analogues of quantum-double non-Abelian defects (beyond Majorana and parafermion zero modes) in electronic systems.

Continuum analogues of zero modes for the abelian
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
case have been identified at interfaces between superconducting and ferromagnetic phases of the edge of a fractional-quantum-Hall medium
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
. The interface is modeled by a segment of the free-boson field theory (
64
), recast as a quantum wire with the help of bosonization
[
19
,
20
,
95
,
97
,
92
,
98
,
21
,
99
]
. The influence of the two phases corresponds to different boundary conditions on the boson’s fields at each end of the segment. The quantum-wire field theory is then solved (see, e.g.,
[
16
]
), and continuum zero modes are identified within the ground-state subspace.

Our strategy is to generalize the above ingredients, namely, the segment of field theory and its boundary conditions, by viewing the original construction through the lens of representation theory. While
we set the stage for generalizing results of the abelian case, we stop
short of solving the resulting theory and constructing zero modes, leaving
such investigations to future work.

6.1
The free boson as a quantum wire

Both the lattice and continuum versions of the clock model can be recast in terms of quantum-wire degrees of freedom, one of which is nonlocal relative to the spin-chain geometry
[
117
,
118
,
119
,
43
,
114
,
115
,
91
]
. The continuum version utilizes
\widehat{\phi}
, corresponding to the order parameter
\widehat{Z}
on the lattice, as well as the collective string excitation

\widehat{\theta}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)=\int^{\mathsf{x}}\text{d}\mathsf{y}\,\widehat{L}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\,,

(84)

which corresponds to the “disorder operator”
\cdots\widehat{X}^{(\mathsf{x}-1)}\widehat{X}^{(\mathsf{x})}
on the lattice (see Sec.
3
). The relative nonlocality between the two “dual” fields is manifest in their commutation relations. Using the canonical commutator between
e^{i\widehat{\phi}}
and
\widehat{L}
,

[e^{i\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)},\widehat{L}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)]=-e^{i\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\delta\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)

(85)

and letting
\Theta
be the Heaviside step function yields
[e^{i\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)},\widehat{\theta}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)]=-e^{i\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\Theta\left(\mathsf{y}-\mathsf{x}\right)
.
When written in terms of
\{\widehat{\phi},\widehat{\theta}\}
, the kinetic energy term in the free-boson field theory becomes
\widehat{L}^{2}\to(\partial\widehat{\theta})^{2}
.

“Quasiparticle” combinations
\exp[i\pi(\widehat{\phi}\pm\widehat{\theta})]
of the dual variables for the
\textnormal{{Z}}_{2}
case represent left- and right-moving Dirac
fermionic fields. These are used to
fermionize
the free boson, i.e., express the quadratic terms of the field theory in terms of the quantum-wire degrees of freedom.
The
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N>2}
case is recast in similar fashion via the slight modification
\pi\to 2\pi/N
in the quasiparticles’ exponential. The resulting operators form an algebra that is equivalent to that of the edge excitations of the fractional quantum Hall phase. Such operators provide a quantum-wire formulation for the general
N
case that is mathematically a close cousin of the
N=2
case (albeit physically being quite different due to the necessary fractional-quantum-Hall bulk).

When in proximity to a ferromagnet, quasiparticles are assumed to interact via a backscattering term
\cos(N\widehat{\phi})
, which we recognize as none other than the
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
pinning potential
\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}
(
65
) of the field theory. The other prominent potential in such systems is a superconducting pairing term

-\text{M}\widehat{V}_{\text{SC}}=-\text{M}\cos\left({\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}\widehat{\theta}}\right)\,,

(86)

“dual” to
\cos(N\widehat{\phi})
in that it pins the collective momentum

\widehat{\theta}
as
\text{M}\to\infty
. Zero modes exist at interfaces between
strongly-pinned ferromagnetic regions (the free boson with
-\text{M}\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}

as
\text{M}\to\infty
) and superconducting regions (the free boson with
-\text{M}\widehat{V}_{\text{SC}}

as
\text{M}\to\infty
)
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
. An
interface between these two regions consists of a segment of the free boson, and the two strongly-pinned
regions manifest themselves as boundary conditions on either side of
the segment [see Fig.
2
(a)]. The free boson is diagonalized
2
, and
continuum zero-mode operators are obtained via the bosonization
techniques we outline below. Such zero modes should be thought of
as continuum analogues of Jordan-Wigner zero modes of the lattice
clock model from Sec.
4
.

Generalizing the zero modes of the
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
case, we would like to realize non-Abelian defects associated with quantum double models on edges of electronic topological phases described by continuum degrees of freedom. Using the continuum mapping of the previous section, we proceed to generalize the ingredients required for the
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
case, namely, the two “dual” potentials and a segment of fermionizable field theory [see Fig.
2
(b)]. For the ferromagnetic
potential
\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}
, we substitute the
G
pinning
term
\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{G}}}
(
132
). In
Sec.
6.2
, we show how a strong pairing potential
\widehat{V}_{\text{SC}}
is equivalent
to projecting onto the quotient subspace
\textnormal{{U}}(1)/\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
, and introduce
an analogous
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
potential that pins sites to
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}/\textnormal{{G}}
.
After reviewing how to fermionize the free boson in Sec.
6.3
,
we show in Sec.
6.4
how to fermionize
the quadratic part of the principal chiral model. Differing from the
abelian case, such fermionization requires an additional Wess-Zumino-Novikov-Witten
term
[
93
,
94
,
95
,
96
]
. This term
is
\overrightarrow{X}^{\textsf{(global)}}
-symmetric — satisfying the symmetry requirements
for belonging to a general quantum-double edge — but we leave identification
of any connections to quantum-double physics to future work.

6.2
Generalized pairing potentials

In the limit of infinitely strong pinning (
\text{M}\to\infty
), the
effect of the superconducting pairing potential (
86
) is equivalent
to that of the local potential

\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{U}}(1)/\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}=\cos\left({\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}}\widehat{L}\right)\,.

(87)

To show this, consider the lattice version of
\widehat{V}_{\text{SC}}
,
acting as
\cos({\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}}\widehat{\theta}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)})

on each site
\mathsf{x}
. Recalling Eq. (
84
),

\widehat{\theta}^{\left(1\right)}=\widehat{L}^{\left(1\right)}
for the first site

\mathsf{x}=1
, so
\widehat{V}_{\text{SC}}^{\left(1\right)}=\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{U}}(1)/\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}{}^{\!\left(1\right)}
.
When the first site is strongly pinned, its momentum
\widehat{L}^{\left(1\right)}

is a multiple of
N
. Plugging this into the potential for the next
site
\mathsf{x}=2
,

\widehat{V}_{\text{SC}}^{\left(\mathsf{x}=2\right)}=\cos\left({\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}}\widehat{\theta}^{\left(2\right)}\right)=\cos\left({\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}}\widehat{L}^{\left(1\right)}+{\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}}\widehat{L}^{\left(2\right)}\right)=\cos\left({\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}}\widehat{L}^{\left(2\right)}\right)=\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{U}}(1)/\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}{}^{\!\left(2\right)}\,.

(88)

The same reasoning holds recursively for sites
\mathsf{x}\geq 3
.

As
\text{M}\to\infty
,
-\text{M}V_{\textnormal{{U}}(1)/\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}
is minimized when the
integer-valued angular momentum
\widehat{L}
is a multiple of
N
. In terms
of rotor momentum states
|\ell\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\text{d}\phi e^{i\ell\phi}|\phi\rangle

[
107
]
, the pinned subspace is thus
\{|\ell=Ns\rangle,s\in\textnormal{{Z}}\}
.
Performing the Fourier transform yields the quotient states (
36
),

\left|a\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}\right\rangle=\sqrt{{\textstyle\frac{N}{2\pi}}}\sum_{s\in\textnormal{{Z}}}e^{-iNsa}\left|\ell=Ns\right\rangle={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}}\sum_{h\in\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}\left|\phi=a+{\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}}h\right\rangle\,,

(89)

labeled by
a\in[0,\frac{2\pi}{N})=\textnormal{{U}}(1)/\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
. This position
state basis reveals that the potential (
87
)
pins the rotor to its
quotient subspace

\textnormal{{U}}(1)/\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
.
A sharper potential uses the sum of projections onto
the basis states
|a\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}\rangle
themselves,

\displaystyle\widehat{V}^{\text{sharp}}_{\textnormal{{U}}(1)/\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\textnormal{{U}}(1)/\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}\text{d}a\left|a\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}\right\rangle\left\langle a\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}\right|={\textstyle\frac{1}{N}}\int_{\textnormal{{U}}(1)}\text{d}\phi\left|\phi\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}\right\rangle\left\langle\phi\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}\right|

(90a)

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{1}{N^{2}}}\sum_{h\in\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{N}h\widehat{L}}\left(\int_{\textnormal{{U}}(1)}\text{d}\phi\left|\phi\right\rangle\left\langle\phi\right|\right)\sum_{k\in\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}e^{i\frac{2\pi}{N}k\widehat{L}}={\textstyle\frac{1}{N}}\sum_{h\in\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{N}h\widehat{L}}\,.

(90b)

This interpretation provides an
avenue for straightforward extension.

The quotient subspace idea naturally generalizes to
\textnormal{{G}}\subset\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
,
where the relevant subspace at each site is spanned by coset states

|a\textnormal{{G}}\rangle
(
36
) with
a\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}/\textnormal{{G}}
. These states are invariant
under
\overleftarrow{X}_{R}
for any
R\in\textnormal{{G}}
, as its action merely reshuffles
the terms in the superposition. The sharp potential — a projection
onto the quotient subspace — can be expressed as an equal
superposition of all
\overleftarrow{X}_{R}

[
123
,
49
]
; following a
derivation similar to that of
\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{U}}(1)/\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}
(
90b
),

\widehat{V}^{\text{sharp}}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}/\textnormal{{G}}}=\int_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}/\textnormal{{G}}}\text{d}a\left|a\textnormal{{G}}\right\rangle\left\langle a\textnormal{{G}}\right|={\textstyle\frac{1}{|\textnormal{{G}}|}}\sum_{R\in\textnormal{{G}}}\overleftarrow{X}_{R}\,.

(91)

For a smooth potential for the
\textnormal{{G}}=\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
case, it suffices to use only the generating elements. Picking a dihedral subgroup according to the prescription from Appx.
C.1
,

\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{SO}}(3)/\textnormal{{G}}}={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\cos\left({\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{z}}\right)+\cos\left(\pi\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{x}}\right)\,.

(92)

These quotient-space potentials convert the principal chiral model associated with
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
(
80
)
into a nonlinear sigma model on
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}/\textnormal{{G}}
. For example, projecting the

\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
model (
136
) onto
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)/\textnormal{{U}}(1)=\textnormal{{S}}^{2}

yields the well-known
\textnormal{{S}}^{2}
nonlinear sigma model (a.k.a. the

\textnormal{{O}}(3)
-rotor model
[
79
]
).

6.3
Abelian bosonization

Bosonization allows one to convert between specific bosonic and fermionic
field theories, stemming from the ability to express fields
\widehat{J}_{\textnormal{KM}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)

(a.k.a.
currents
or
vertex operators
) of a certain Lie algebra — a
Kac-Moody
(KM) algebra
2
2

2

The field
\widehat{J}_{\textnormal{KM}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)
is the generating
series
[
124
]
of the KM algebra. With
\mathsf{x}
assumed to lie
on a circle, the actual KM algebra generators are its integer-labeled
Fourier components
[
98
,
99
,
125
]
; these components
are the “normal modes” that diagonalize the quantum wire. Their algebra is infinite-dimensional
(as opposed to the angular momentum algebra, which is three-dimensional).

— in terms of either bosonic or fermionic fields. Because of this,
any bosonic fields satisfying the commutation relations of KM currents
can also be written in terms of fermionic fields, and vice versa.
This ability allows one to interpret the same Hilbert space in two
remarkably different ways — either as the space of excitations
of bosons or of fermions.

A level-
\kappa

\textnormal{{U}}(1)
KM algebra corresponds to one current with
commutation relation

\left[\widehat{J}_{\textnormal{KM}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right),\widehat{J}_{\textnormal{KM}}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\right]=i\frac{\kappa}{2\pi}\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\delta\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)\equiv i\frac{\kappa}{2\pi}\delta^{\prime}\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)\,,

(93)

where the integer parameter
\kappa
is called the
level
. The

\delta
-function derivative may seem unruly to the uninitiated, but
we soon show that, on the bosonic side, it arises naturally due to
presence of the derivative in the current
\partial\widehat{\phi}
.

We need two copies of a
\textnormal{{U}}(1)
KM algebra,
\widehat{J}_{\textnormal{KM}}^{\pm}
with levels

\kappa=\pm 1
, to fermionize the free boson
(
64
). We show that superpositions of the fields

\partial\widehat{\phi},\widehat{L}
are exactly these currents. Recalling the canonical commutator (
85
) and applying
\partial
yields

\left[\partial e^{i\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)},\widehat{L}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\right]=\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\left[e^{i\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)},\widehat{L}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\right]=-\left(\partial_{\mathsf{x}}e^{i\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\right)\delta\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)-e^{i\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\delta^{\prime}\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)\,.

(94)

Expressing
\partial\widehat{\phi}=-ie^{-i\widehat{\phi}}\partial e^{i\widehat{\phi}}
, using the commutator identity
[A,BC]=B[A,C]+[A,B]C
,
and plugging in the above yields

\big[\partial\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{x}\right),\widehat{L}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\big]=-i\big[e^{-i\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\partial_{\mathsf{x}}e^{i\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)},\widehat{L}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\big]=i\delta^{\prime}\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)\,.

(95)

The
\delta
-functions cancel, leaving only the required

\delta^{\prime}
.
Defining

\widehat{J}_{\textnormal{KM}}^{\pm}={\textstyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}}(\partial\widehat{\phi}\pm\widehat{L})

(96)

yields the desired relations (
93
) for each
current. These currents can then be used to express the free boson
(
64
).

To complete the dictionary, we express the KM currents as fermionic
bilinears. Let
\widehat{\psi}_{\pm}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)
be left- and right-moving Dirac
fermion fields, satisfying canonical anti-commutation relations
\{\widehat{\psi}_{p}\left(\mathsf{x}\right),\widehat{\psi}_{q}^{\dagger}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\}=\delta_{pq}\delta(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y})

and
\{\widehat{\psi}_{p}\left(\mathsf{x}\right),\widehat{\psi}_{q}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\}=0
for
p,q\in\pm
.
To show that the bilinears
\widehat{\psi}_{\pm}^{\dagger}\widehat{\psi}_{\pm}
are indeed
representations of the KM currents (
93
), we
have to take care of infinities arising from products of
\mathsf{t}
ime-evolved
fields (
[
98
]
, Sec. 2.II):

\widehat{\psi}_{p}\left(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{t}\right)\widehat{\psi}_{q}^{\dagger}\left(\mathsf{y},0\right)\sim\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{\delta_{pq}}{\mathsf{t}-i\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{as $\mathsf{y}\to\mathsf{x}$ and $\mathsf{t}\to 0$\,.}

(97)

This divergence is the reason behind the required
\delta^{\prime}
— the
“chiral anomaly”
[
98
]
or “Schwinger term”
[
126
,
99
]
— in
the equal-time commutator of the fermion bilinears.

The bosonization dictionary allows us to fermionize
\partial\widehat{\phi},\widehat{L}
as
well as the group field
e^{i\widehat{\phi}}
:

{\textstyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}}\left(\partial\widehat{\phi}+\widehat{L}\right)\leftrightarrow\widehat{\psi}_{+}^{\dagger}\widehat{\psi}_{+}\,\,\,;\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}}\left(\partial\widehat{\phi}-\widehat{L}\right)\leftrightarrow\widehat{\psi}_{-}^{\dagger}\widehat{\psi}_{-}\,\,\,;\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\textstyle\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}}e^{i\widehat{\phi}}\leftrightarrow\widehat{\psi}_{+}^{\dagger}\widehat{\psi}_{-}\,.

(98)

These fermionic fields can alternatively be expressed as exponentials of
\widehat{\phi}
and the nonlocal
\widehat{\theta}
field (
84
).

6.4
Non-Abelian bosonization

Here we apply a “non-abelian” version of the bosonization procedure
from the previous section to determine when the quadratic terms in
the principal chiral model (
80
) can be expressed in
terms of fermions. This version uses two copies of a
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
Kac-Moody
(KM) algebra at levels
\pm\kappa
, whose “left” and “right”
currents respectively satisfy

\displaystyle\left[\overrightarrow{J}_{KM}^{\texttt{a}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right),\overrightarrow{J}_{KM}^{\texttt{b}}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=if_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}\overrightarrow{J}_{KM}^{\texttt{c}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\delta\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)+i\frac{\kappa}{4\pi}\delta_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\delta^{\prime}\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)

(99a)

\displaystyle\left[\overleftarrow{J}_{KM}^{\texttt{a}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right),\overleftarrow{J}_{KM}^{\texttt{b}}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=if_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}\overleftarrow{J}_{KM}^{\texttt{c}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\delta\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)-i\frac{\kappa}{4\pi}\delta_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\delta^{\prime}\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)\,,

(99b)

while commuting with each other. We let
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
be either the special unitary or special orthogonal group, with structure constants
f_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}
. As before, these currents have representations in terms of the bosonic fields of the principal chiral model as well as fermionic fields.

Mimicking Sec.
6.3
, we
proceed to identify the
\delta^{\prime}

term required for the KM currents. Since we are in a non-Abelian case,
\partial U.U^{\dagger}\neq U^{\dagger}.\partial U
, so there is a position current complementary to
\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}
(
79
):

\overrightarrow{\partial\phi}_{\ell}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)=i\partial\widehat{U}_{\ell}.\widehat{U}_{\ell}^{\dagger}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)=\overrightarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{a}}L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{with}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overrightarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{a}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)={\textstyle\frac{1}{c_{\ell}}}\textsc{Tr}\big[\overrightarrow{\partial\phi}_{\ell}\left(\mathsf{x}\right).L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}}\big]\,.

(100)

We summarize the algebra of formed by
\{\overrightarrow{\partial\phi},\overleftarrow{\partial\phi},\overrightarrow{L},\overleftarrow{L}\}
in Table
5
, describing some relevant parts below.

Lie-algebraic versions of the Weyl-type relations (
15
),
obtained by expanding
\{\overrightarrow{X},\overleftarrow{X}\}
in terms of
\{\overrightarrow{L},\overleftarrow{L}\}
(
76
),
are

\displaystyle\big[\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right),\widehat{U}_{\ell}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\big]

\displaystyle=-L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}}.\widehat{U}_{\ell}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\delta\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)

(101a)

\displaystyle\big[\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right),\widehat{U}_{\ell}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\big]

\displaystyle=+\widehat{U}_{\ell}\left(\mathsf{x}\right).L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}}\,\delta\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)\,.

(101b)

To determine the commutator of
\overrightarrow{L}
and
\overrightarrow{\partial\phi}
, we generalize the

), using Hilbert-Schmidt

(

,
with the crucial
\delta^{\prime}

\overrightarrow{\partial\phi}

[\overleftarrow{L},\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}]
in the table.

Commutation
