Rounding out Table
5
,
the calculation of the “cross” commutator
[\overleftarrow{L},\overrightarrow{\partial\phi}]
involves

\widehat{R}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}=(\widehat{R}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}})^{\dagger}
, which yields the
real-valued matrix element
R_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}
of the adjoint representation from Eq. (
83
) when applied to a state
|R\rangle
for
R\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
. This operator converts between left and right currents
[
51
,
127
]
,

\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}=-\widehat{R}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{b}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overrightarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{a}}=-\widehat{R}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{b}}\,.

(102)

By defining momentum currents
\overrightarrow{L}_{\ell}\equiv\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}}

and
\overleftarrow{L}_{\ell}=\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}}
, analogously to
\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}
(
79
)
and
\overrightarrow{\partial\phi}
(
100
), the above conversion
is equivalent to
\overrightarrow{L}_{\ell}=-\widehat{U}_{\ell}.\overleftarrow{L}_{\ell}.\widehat{U}_{\ell}^{\dagger}

and
\overrightarrow{\partial\phi}_{\ell}=-\widehat{U}_{\ell}.\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}_{\ell}.\widehat{U}_{\ell}^{\dagger}
. This relationship
between the currents makes contact with the classical version of our
field theory, where left currents
\overrightarrow{L}_{\ell}\leftrightarrow i\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\widehat{U}_{\ell}.\widehat{U}_{\ell}^{\dagger}

are the time-like versions of the space-like
\overrightarrow{\partial\phi}_{\ell}\leftrightarrow i\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\widehat{U}_{\ell}.\widehat{U}_{\ell}^{\dagger}

(and similarly for the right currents). The Poisson brackets of all
currents (
[
128
]
, Part Two, Ch. 1, Sec. 5) are identical
to the commutators from Table
5
. This correspondence
serves as a key starting point for constructing the KM currents.

With the
\delta^{\prime}
in hand, one final ingredient is required to
identify the KM currents for this case, namely, the commutators in
Table
5
have to
be slightly modified. On the classical Lagrangian level, this modification
occurs to the Poisson brackets of the angular momenta
\{\overrightarrow{L},\overleftarrow{L}\}

(
[
128
]
, Part Two, Ch. 1, Sec. 5), and stems from an additional
Wess-Zumino-Novikov-Witten (WZNW) term
[
93
,
94
,
95
,
96
]

that converts the principal chiral model into a
WZNW model
. On
the quantum level, this translates to modifying the momentum commutators
(
76
) to

\displaystyle\big[\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right),\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{b}}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\big]

\displaystyle=if_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}\left(\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{c}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)-{\textstyle\frac{\kappa}{8\pi}}\overrightarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{c}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\right)\delta\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)

(103a)

\displaystyle\big[\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right),\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{b}}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\big]

\displaystyle=if_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}\left(\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{c}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)+{\textstyle\frac{\kappa}{8\pi}}\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{c}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\right)\delta\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)\,.

(103b)

All other commutators remain the same. In
order to fermionize, we have to assume that the angular momenta
\{\overrightarrow{L},\overleftarrow{L}\}

used to construct the field theory (
80
) satisfy
these relations. With this assumption, the following superpositions
satisfy the KM relations (
99
),

\overrightarrow{J}_{KM}^{\texttt{a}}=\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}+{\textstyle\frac{\kappa}{8\pi}}\overrightarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{a}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overleftarrow{J}_{KM}^{\texttt{a}}=\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}-{\textstyle\frac{\kappa}{8\pi}}\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{a}}\,.

(104)

We now turn to the fermionic side. Expressing KM currents as fermion bilinears requires
M
species [recall that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is either
\textnormal{{SO}}(M)
or
\textnormal{{SU}}(M)
] of left and right
fermion operators
\{\overrightarrow{\psi}_{m},\overleftarrow{\psi}_{m}\}
. These satisfy

\big\{\overrightarrow{\psi}_{m}\left(\mathsf{x}\right),\overrightarrow{\psi}_{n}^{\dagger}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\big\}=\delta_{mn}\delta\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\big\{\overleftarrow{\psi}_{m}\left(\mathsf{x}\right),\overleftarrow{\psi}_{n}^{\dagger}\left(\mathsf{y}\right)\big\}=\delta_{mn}\delta\left(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\right)\,,

(105)

with all others anticommuting with each other. Recalling from Sec.
6.3

the nuances of fermionic products, the fermionic representation is
[
97
,
98
,
99
]

\overrightarrow{J}_{KM}^{\texttt{a}}\leftrightarrow\sum_{m,n}\overrightarrow{\psi}_{m}^{\dagger}[L_{\ell=1}^{\texttt{a}}]_{mn}\overrightarrow{\psi}_{n}\equiv\overrightarrow{\psi}^{\dagger}.L_{1}^{\texttt{a}}.\overrightarrow{\psi}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,;\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overleftarrow{J}_{KM}^{\texttt{a}}\leftrightarrow\overleftarrow{\psi}^{\dagger}.L_{1}^{\texttt{a}}.\overleftarrow{\psi}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{;}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,[\widehat{U}_{1}]_{mn}\leftrightarrow\overrightarrow{\psi}_{m}^{\dagger}\overleftarrow{\psi}_{n},

(106)

where
\ell=1
is the
defining irrep
of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
(e.g., for
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}=\textnormal{{SO}}(M)
, the
M
-dimensional irrep of orthogonal rotation matrices).

6.5
Generalized interfaces

The
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
principal chiral model is not fermionizable for all parameters
\{\text{C},\text{f}\}
, unlike the free boson. We have, however, identified a point
({\textstyle\frac{8\pi}{\kappa}})^{2}\text{C}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}=\text{f}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}=\delta_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}
for which the quadratic terms of the theory can be recast in terms of fermions; we describe this procedure below. We believe that this point is related to the self-dual edge case
\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{G}}}^{\text{sd}}
of the flux ladder lattice model (see Sec.
3.6
and Table
4
).

The principal chiral model at the critical point consists of products
(\overleftarrow{L})^{2}
and
(\overleftarrow{\partial\phi})^{2}
, while the KM generators contain both
\{\overleftarrow{L},\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}\}
and
\{\overrightarrow{L},\overrightarrow{\partial\phi}\}
. However, convertibility (
102
)
between currents, along with Hermiticity of all operators
involved, allows us to “change arrows” of any summed quadratic
combination of
A,B\in\{L,J\}
,

\overrightarrow{A}^{\texttt{a}}\overrightarrow{B}^{\texttt{a}}=\widehat{R}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\overleftarrow{A}^{\texttt{b}}\widehat{R}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{c}}\overleftarrow{B}^{\texttt{c}}=\overleftarrow{A}^{\texttt{b}}\widehat{R}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\widehat{R}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{c}}\overleftarrow{B}^{\texttt{c}}=\overleftarrow{A}^{\texttt{b}}\widehat{R^{-1}}_{\texttt{b}\texttt{a}}\widehat{R}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{c}}\overleftarrow{B}^{\texttt{c}}=\overleftarrow{A}^{\texttt{b}}\delta_{\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}\overleftarrow{B}^{\texttt{c}}=\overleftarrow{A}^{\texttt{a}}\overleftarrow{B}^{\texttt{a}}\,.

(107)

This allows us to express the continuum theory at
({\textstyle\frac{8\pi}{\kappa}})^{2}\text{C}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}=\text{f}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}=\delta_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}
in terms of the KM currents,

\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{G}}}^{\text{sd}}\to\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}+\left({\textstyle\frac{\kappa}{8\pi}}\right)^{2}\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{a}}\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{a}}={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\big(\overrightarrow{J}_{KM}^{\texttt{a}}\overrightarrow{J}_{KM}^{\texttt{a}}+\overleftarrow{J}_{KM}^{\texttt{a}}\overleftarrow{J}_{KM}^{\texttt{a}}\big)\,.

(108)

This, in turn can be expressed in terms of the fermionic degrees of freedom using Eq. (
106
). One can also diagonalize the model by expressing the KM currents in terms of normal modes.
2

Using Ref.
[
114
]
, one can equivalently express the KM generators in terms of
\{\overleftarrow{\partial\phi},\overrightarrow{\partial\phi}\}
and non-Abelian generalizations of the nonlocal
\widehat{\theta}
-field from Eq. (
84
),

\overleftarrow{\theta}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)=\int^{\mathsf{x}}\text{d}\mathsf{y}\,\overleftarrow{L}(\mathsf{y})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overrightarrow{\theta}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)=\int^{\mathsf{x}}\text{d}\mathsf{y}\,\overrightarrow{L}(\mathsf{y})~.

(109)

Note that
\overrightarrow{\theta}
is a continuum analogue of the disorder-like anyonic excitation from Eq. (
4.3
).

We conclude this section by conjecturing that there exists a combination of boundary conditions on the fields of the
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
WZNW model (
108
) that yields a degenerate ground state space, along with zero-mode operators that permute the different ground states. There are many combinations possible: one can pick various subgroups
\textnormal{{G}}\subset\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
for either end of a wire of length
\mathsf{L}
, and boundary conditions may be stated in terms of various field pairs
(
[
128
]
, Part Two, Ch. 1, Sec. 5). For example, one can pin the
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
-valued field
\widehat{U}(\mathsf{x})
to a subgroup
\textnormal{{G}}\subset\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
at
\mathsf{x}=0
, while pinning the “dual” field
\overrightarrow{\theta}(\mathsf{x})
to the quotient space
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}/\textnormal{{G}}
on the other end
\mathsf{x}=\mathsf{L}
of the wire. In the abelian case
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}=\textnormal{{U}}(1)
and
\textnormal{{G}}=\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
, one obtains the parafermionic zero modes at the interface between superconducting and ferromagnetic regions of the fermionized free boson
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
. When
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}=\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
and
\textnormal{{G}}=\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
, the dual-field boundary condition corresponds to pinning the angular momentum
\ell
to be zero modulo
N
, similar to the bulk ground-state constraint of the flux-ladder lattice model. We leave verification of this conjecture to future work.

7
Discussion & future directions

The 1D quantum Ising model can be interpreted in three different ways:
as an ordinary spin chain, as a superconducting fermionic chain, or
as a standalone effective edge theory for the surface code. This model
and its continuum description thus lie at a key intersection of a
wide variety of topics in the theory of topological order. Motivated by realizing more exotic topological
order associated with non-Abelian finite groups
G
for the purpose of
intrinsically protected quantum computation, we derive an analogue
of the quantum Ising model for quantum doubles. We develop both a lattice effective theory for the quantum-double
edge as well as its continuum description. While based on the mathematical framework of representation theory, our extensions nevertheless establish a starting point for potentially realizing exotic topological order in known physical systems. With our results described in detail in Sec.
2
, here we focus on giving broader context and identifying future directions.

On the lattice front, we show that a slight generalization of a previously studied model called the
flux ladder

[
71
]
describes the edge of the quantum double model, provided that the bulk has some fixed anyonic charge.
To extract the flux ladder from the quantum-double edge, we recast the quantum-double Hamiltonian in terms of generalized “stabilizers” and manipulate said stabilizers in a similar fashion as is done in conventional stabilizer-based error correction. Such techniques may extend tools from stabilizer-based error correction to these and other exotic topological phases such as Hopf-algebra based quantum double models
[
129
]
and string-nets
[
130
,
131
,
132
]
, which circumvent various stabilizer-based no-go theorems
[
133
,
134
,
135
]
and may offer a native universal set of protected gates.

On the continuum front, we provide a recipe for obtaining a field theory from a lattice model associated with
G
by embedding
G
into a Lie group and taking the continuum limit. Symmetries and parameters are naturally preserved during this embedding, and field theories describing low-energy excitations of previously known lattice models can be obtained from said models via this procedure. Applying this to the edge and bulk of quantum double models, we respectively obtain generalized versions of the 1D principal chiral model and 2D non-Abelian gauge theory. Since we consider general (and not necessarily gapped) edges, the parameter space of the obtained edge theory turns out to be quite large. We find a special point in the parameter space at which the edge field theory can be fermionized via non-Abelian bosonization and addition of a Wess-Zumino-Novikov-Witten (WZNW) term. This procedure provides a starting point for extending constructions of continuum parafermion modes in electronic systems to continuum versions of quantum-double defects
[
16
]
.

Our work is necessarily technical due to the nuances of non-Abelian groups, calling for extensive derivation of statements that require little work in the
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
case. As a result, there is much left to be done, and we outline some potentially interesting directions below.

Stability of
G
-type zero modes

While parafermionic (
\textnormal{{G}}=\textnormal{{Z}}_{N>2}
) strong zero modes are generically not robust against transverse-field perturbations, there exist fine-tuned points in the parameter space of the clock model at which strong zero modes are purported to exist
[
87
,
88
]
.
At those special “integrable” points, the eigenspectrum of the longitudinal part of the clock model happens to be invariant under a sign flip,
\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}^{\text{edge}}\to-\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}^{\text{edge}}

[
47
]
. We have identified a point in the parameter space of the dihedral (
\textnormal{{G}}=\textnormal{{D}}_{3}
) flux ladder with the same property (see Appx.
B
), and our preliminary numerical investigations hint at similar robustness of zero modes at such a point. It would be useful to perform a thorough numerical treatment and identify any connections of this and other special points to integrability.

Continuum
G
-type defects

While we provide a plausible group-theoretic extension of the machinery that produces parafermionic (i.e.,
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
-type) defects on edges of a quantum wire that models a fractional quantum-Hall medium, we do not construct the defects themselves. A path toward such a construction likely requires expressing the bosonized quantum wire in terms of normal modes
2
, and imposing various boundary conditions on said modes. It may also be fruitful to express the quantum-wire fields in terms continuum non-Abelian order/disorder fields [see Eq. (
109
)]
[
114
]
, akin to expressing parafermionic operators in terms of the well-known
\widehat{\theta},\widehat{\phi}
fields. On a similar note, we have shown that simplified versions of our ribbons behave similarly to the
lattice
order/disorder operators of the clock model
[
113
,
114
,
115
]
. Relating any discovered continuum defects to our flattened ribbons and order/disorder operators could be especially illuminating.

Interpreting the WZNW term

Addition of this term, necessary for fermionization of the continuum edge theory, is allowed in the sense that it does not violate the bulk ground-state constraint imposed by the theory’s global symmetry. Nevertheless, the term’s relation to quantum-double edges and anyonic condensation remains unclear. One possible path toward clarity could be to investigate more general gapped edges constructed from projective representations
[
62
]
. This could yield connections to the WZNW term because its addition also yields a projective representation (of a group that is closely related to the Lie group defining the continuum theory
[
97
]
). Of course, the presence of projective representations in both contexts could also be coincidental.

Symmetry-protected topological phases

We have identified a set of commuting-projector instances of the flux ladder that admit symmetry breaking of the parent group
G
into any subgroup
K
[see Eq. (
33
)], generalizing analogous constructions for the abelian case (
\textnormal{{G}}=\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
)
[
110
,
111
,
112
]
. We note, however, that these may not be the only phases of the flux ladder, and that SPT order may also exist. Since projective representations are used to classify SPTs, such phases could exist in flux ladders constructed out of
X
-type operators that admit a projective representation of
K
.

Non-Abelian dualities

A category-theoretical duality known as “Morita equivalence”
[
10
,
116
]
promises to generalize the well-known Kramers-Wannier duality of the clock model. This correspondence allows one to map between different Morita-equivalent gapped quantum-double edge instances of the flux ladder, such as the pure-charge and pure-flux edges comprising the model’s “self-dual” instance (see Sec.
3.6
). While there has been some work in formulating such duality transformations for lattice models
[
116
]
, an explicit realization applicable to the flux ladder remains to be done. Doing so would provide a natural set of dualities for non-Abelian group models, which have so far proven difficult to construct
[
118
,
53
,
136
,
137
,
138
,
127
,
139
,
140
,
141
,
28
,
116
,
142
]
.

Category-theoretic classifications
[
62
,
63
,
64
,
65
,
69
,
70
,
29
]

While we verify that the algebra of our flattened ribbons is indeed the same as that of ordinary ribbons [see Eq. (
49
)], elements of our ribbon basis can carry multiple edge excitations. A more complete treatment could explicitly construct the proper ribbon superpositions that carry single edge excitations, identify their braiding relations, and investigate interplay with the gapped-edge instances of the flux ladder.

Appendices

Appendix A
Effect of bulk excitations on the edge theory

While we have focused on the quantum-double bulk being in a ground
state when deriving our effective edge models, here we discuss how
the edge lattice model is modified when nontrivial bulk excitations
are present. We also conjecture how such excitations are transferred
to the continuum.

A.1
Charge excitations

For the qunit surface code, the bulk ground-state constraint projects
the effective edge clock model (
9
) into the
subspace where the star term
\widehat{X}_{h}^{\textsf{(global)}}
(
12
)
is identity. More general bulk excitations correspond to projecting
the edge model into a subspace where the star is a root of unity
e^{i\frac{2\pi}{N}\lambda}
,
with
\lambda\in\{0,1,\cdots,N-1\}
. The projections defining such subspaces
are

\widehat{P}_{\lambda}={\textstyle\frac{1}{N}}\sum_{h\in\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{N}\lambda h}\widehat{X}_{h}^{\textsf{(global)}}\,.

(110)

A pair of bulk charge excitations for quantum doubles is
labeled by irreps
\lambda
of
G
and is created by the operator

\textsc{Tr}[\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}]
acting on a bulk site
[
143
,
2
]

(akin to Pauli
\widehat{Z}
matrices creating charge excitations for the surface code).
Charge excitations correspond to projecting the flux ladder (
27
)
onto the subspace corresponding to the irrep
\lambda
present in the
representation of
G
formed by
\overrightarrow{X}_{h\in\textnormal{{G}}}^{\textsf{(global)}}

(
31
). The corresponding projection is

\overrightarrow{P}_{\lambda}={\textstyle\frac{d_{\lambda}}{|\textnormal{{G}}|}}\sum_{h\in\textnormal{{G}}}\textsc{Tr}\left[\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}\left(h^{-1}\right)\right]\overrightarrow{X}_{h}^{\textsf{(global)}}\,.

(111)

For the flux ladder, excitations are carried by the order-parameter operator
\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{1,\lambda}=\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}
(
38
).

(113)

The extra phase is a
topological boundary condition
on the
chain
[
112
]
, and our derivation relates such
conditions to the presence of bulk flux excitations.

In the continuum limit, the above condition can be rephrased as a
condition on the field

\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)
. Let us parameterize the continuum edge with
spatial index
\mathsf{x}\in[0,\mathsf{L})
, and place the violated spoke such that

(\mathsf{x}-1,\mathsf{x})\to(\mathsf{L},0)
in the limit. The above constraint is then
equivalent to
\widehat{\phi}\left(\mathsf{L}\right)=\widehat{\phi}\left(0\right)+h
.

The generalization to quantum doubles, where flux excitations are labeled by conjugacy classes of
G
, is straightforward. With
h\in\text{cls}(h)
a representative of the conjugacy class of
h
, a
local bulk plaquette constraint (
24
)
on a section of tire generalizes to

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.82999pt}{-2.85004pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\big]=\textsc{Tr}\big[\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}_{\lambda}(h)\big]\,.

(114)

This constraint, when plugged into Eq. (
26
),
yields the modified two-body term at the relevant site,

\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}-1\right)}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\to\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}-1\right)}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$Z$\cr}}}(h).\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\,.

(115)

This modification can also be done by conjugating the Hamiltonian with the “disorder” operator
\overrightarrow{\varGamma}_{h,\boldsymbol{1}}^{(2\mathsf{x})}
(
38
). We anticipate that, in the continuum limit of the corresponding principal
chiral model (
80
), the above constraint is related
to the
monodromy condition

[
81
]
on the
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
-valued
field
\widehat{U}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)
, i.e.,
\widehat{U}\left(\mathsf{x}=\mathsf{L}\right)=U(h).\widehat{U}\left(\mathsf{x}=0\right)
.

A.3
Combinations

General excitations in quantum doubles are labeled by
\{\varLambda,\text{cls}(h)\}
,
where
\varLambda\in\text{irr}(\textnormal{{C}}(h))
, and
\textnormal{{C}}(h)
is the
centralizer

— the subgroup of all elements that commute with
h
. Such
a general bulk excitation translates simultaneously to a global subspace
constraint and a twisted boundary condition in our effective edge
model (
27
). The edge is projected onto the global
subspace with projection

\overrightarrow{P}_{\varLambda}={\textstyle\frac{1}{|\textnormal{{C}}(h)|}}\sum_{b\in\textnormal{{C}}(h)}\chi_{\varLambda}\left(b\right)\overrightarrow{X}_{b}^{\textsf{(global)}}\,,

(116)

where
|\textnormal{{C}}(h)|
is the order of
\textnormal{{C}}(h)
, and
\chi_{\varLambda}

is the character of
h
in the irrep
\varLambda
of the centralizer.
The twisted boundary condition yields a two-body term that is modified
by the representative element a la Eq. (
115
).

Appendix B
Dihedral flux ladder

B.1
Explicit construction of dihedral operators

Each element
h\in\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
can be expressed as
h=r^{n}p^{{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(1-\mu)}
,
where
n\in\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
, and
\mu\in\pm 1
denotes either the absence or
presence of the reflection. With
(n,\mu)
uniquely determining each
element, each site
\mathsf{x}
can be interpreted as a tensor product of
a qu
N
it
\{|n\rangle\,,\,n\in\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}\}
and a qubit
\{\left|\pm\right\rangle\}
.
Following Eqs. (
13
) and dihedral multiplication
rules, the left- and right-multipliers act on this space as

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\begin{aligned} \overrightarrow{X}_{r}\left|n,\mu\right\rangle&=\left|n+1,\mu\right\rangle\\
\overrightarrow{X}_{p}|n,\mu\rangle&=\left|-n,-\mu\right\rangle\end{aligned}&&\begin{aligned} \overleftarrow{X}_{r}\left|n,\mu\right\rangle&=\left|n-\mu,\mu\right\rangle\\
\overleftarrow{X}_{p}|n,\mu\rangle&=\left|n,-\mu\right\rangle\end{aligned}\end{array}\,.

(117)

We can construct these explicitly using the qunit Pauli operator
\widehat{X}
,
the qunit “reflection” operator
\widehat{S}
(with
\widehat{S}\widehat{X}\widehat{S}=\widehat{X}^{\dagger}

and
\widehat{S}\widehat{Z}\widehat{S}=\widehat{Z}^{\dagger}
), as well as the associated qubit operators

\widehat{\sigma}_{x},\widehat{\sigma}_{z}
, and
\widehat{P}_{\pm}=\left|\pm\right\rangle\left\langle\pm\right|
(cf.
[
144
,
145
,
71
]
):

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\begin{aligned} \overrightarrow{X}_{r}&=\widehat{X}\otimes 1\\
\overrightarrow{X}_{p}&=\widehat{S}\otimes\widehat{\sigma}_{x}\end{aligned}&&\begin{aligned} \overleftarrow{X}_{r}&=\widehat{X}^{\dagger}\otimes\widehat{P}_{+}+\widehat{X}\otimes\widehat{P}_{-}\\
\overleftarrow{X}_{p}&=1\otimes\widehat{\sigma}_{x}\end{aligned}\end{array}\,.

(118)

The diagonal operators can be constructed out of the qunit/qubit
operators
\widehat{Z},\widehat{\sigma}_{z},\widehat{P}_{\pm}
:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\begin{aligned} \text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\boldsymbol{1}}&=1\\
\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}}&=1\otimes\widehat{\sigma}_{z}\end{aligned}&&\begin{aligned} \text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\boldsymbol{1}^{+}}&=\widehat{Z}^{N/2}\otimes 1\\
\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\boldsymbol{1}^{-}}&=\widehat{Z}^{N/2}\otimes\widehat{\sigma}_{z}\end{aligned}\end{array}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\boldsymbol{2}_{n}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\widehat{Z}^{n}\otimes\widehat{P}_{+}&\widehat{Z}^{n}\otimes\widehat{P}_{-}\\
\widehat{Z}^{n\dagger}\otimes\widehat{P}_{-}&\widehat{Z}^{n\dagger}\otimes\widehat{P}_{+}\end{array}\right]\,.

(119)

These satisfy the relation (
14
) when acting
on the group states
|h\rangle\leftrightarrow|n,\mu\rangle
.

Flux-ladder
ZZ
-terms corresponding to 1D-irreps
\lambda
are simple products
of qunit/qubit operators; e.g.,
\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\dagger}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}=\widehat{\sigma}_{z}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\widehat{\sigma}_{z}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}

is an Ising interaction. The 2D-irrep term is

\displaystyle\textsc{Tr}[\text{C}_{\boldsymbol{2}_{n}}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\boldsymbol{2}_{n}}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\boldsymbol{2}_{n}}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}]=

\displaystyle\text{C}_{\boldsymbol{2}_{n}}^{11}\left(\widehat{Z}_{n}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\widehat{Z}_{n}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\otimes\widehat{P}_{+}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\widehat{P}_{+}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}+\widehat{Z}_{n}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\widehat{Z}_{n}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\otimes\widehat{P}_{-}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\widehat{P}_{-}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\right)+\text{h.c.}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\text{C}_{\boldsymbol{2}_{n}}^{12}\left(\widehat{Z}_{n}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\widehat{Z}_{n}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\otimes\widehat{P}_{-}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\widehat{P}_{+}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}+\widehat{Z}_{n}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\widehat{Z}_{n}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\otimes\widehat{P}_{+}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\widehat{P}_{-}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\right)+\text{h.c.}\,,

(124)

where we have moved
n
downstairs for compactness. The one-body
X
-type
term is

\sum_{n\in\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}\text{f}_{r^{n}}\left(\widehat{X}_{n}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\otimes\widehat{P}_{+}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}+\widehat{X}_{n}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\otimes\widehat{P}_{-}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\right)+\text{h.c.}+\text{f}_{r^{n}p}\left(\widehat{X}_{n}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\otimes\widehat{\sigma}_{+}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}+\widehat{X}_{n}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\otimes\widehat{\sigma}_{-}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\right)\,.

(125)

,

,

,

,

,

) for

B.2
Notable cases of the flux ladder

Let us briefly go through the different specific cases of the model
(
27
) from Table
6
, with
exception of the
q-double
case covered in Sec.
3.5
.
In order to contrast with the clock model (
9
),
we identify which strings of permutation matrices leave
\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}^{\text{edge}}

invariant in each case. For
general
coefficients, we believe
the only such symmetry is the
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
symmetry already mentioned.
This was verified numerically for
\textnormal{{D}}_{3}
and
\textnormal{{D}}_{4}
by randomly sampling
coefficients
\text{C},\text{f}
and explicitly checking commutation for all

\left(2N\right)!
strings. The same holds when
C
is
Hermitian

and
f
is real.

The
chiral
case
[
71
]
assumes a unitary
C
;
given the general conditions, this happens when either
\text{C}_{\lambda}^{11}=0

or
\text{C}_{\lambda}^{12}=0
. For the latter case, the permutation symmetry
is the same as before.

In the
Gauge theory
case, the symmetry enlarges further to

\textnormal{{D}}_{N}\times\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
, represented by products of
\overrightarrow{X}^{\textsf{(global)}}
and

\overleftarrow{X}^{\textsf{(global)}}
. The two-body term is
\textsc{Tr}[\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\dagger}.\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\lambda}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}]

(akin to the the two-body term in the principal chiral model), while

\text{f}_{h}
depends only on the conjugacy class of
h
. An additional
“inversion” symmetry
\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}\to\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}^{\dagger}
and
\overrightarrow{X}\to\overleftarrow{X}
is also
present. This case is a finite-group version of Kogut-Susskind gauge theory on an exotic Hawaiian earring geometry
[
86
]
.

The dihedral structure of the local Hilbert space is not necessary to describe the
Self-dual
case, which is equivalent to the self-dual point of the
\textnormal{{Z}}_{2N}
clock model (see Sec.
3.6
). We suspect
the same is true for the
Gauge theory
case, at least for
\textnormal{{D}}_{3}
, as we have numerically
verified that the two-body term can be expressed in
terms of
\textnormal{{Z}}_{6}
-clock-model
Z
operators (generated by
\widehat{Z}\otimes\widehat{\sigma}_{z}

on each site).

In the
Integrable?
case for
\textnormal{{D}}_{N=3}
, the spectrum of
the two-body term is symmetric around a particular value (which can
be shifted to zero by adding a constant). For the clock model, such
a special point is relevant in studying the stability of parafermionic
zero modes
[
87
,
88
]
, and signals that there may
be integrable points
[
47
]
. We leave this to
future work.

Appendix C
Explicit
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
embedding procedure

Recall that the dihedral group
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}=\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}\rtimes\textnormal{{Z}}_{2}
,
where
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}=\left\langle r\right\rangle
is the rotation subgroup,
and
\textnormal{{Z}}_{2}=\left\langle p\right\rangle
is the reflection subgroup.
In order to take continuum limits, we would like to embed this group
into a larger group space such that any pair of elements
h,k\in\textnormal{{D}}_{N}

can be connected to each other via a continuous path. A space satisfying this criteria is
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
, the group of 3D proper
rotations.

C.1
Dihedral to
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
flux ladder

The structure of the
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
group-space operators is much like that of

\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
: the space consists of element-labeled basis vectors,
\{|R\rangle\,,\,R\in\textnormal{{SO}}(3)\}
,
while left (
\overrightarrow{X}_{R}
) and right (
\overleftarrow{X}_{R}
) rotations act by permuting
basis elements according to the group multiplication rules (
13
).
We can directly embed our left and right dihedral multipliers for

h\in\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
as
\overrightarrow{X}_{h}\to\overrightarrow{X}_{R=h}
and
\overleftarrow{X}_{h}\to\overleftarrow{X}_{R=h}
,
where in the latter formulas
h\in\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
is treated as an element
of a dihedral subgroup of
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
. The notation for
X
-operators
is thus the same for both groups; we will make it clear from context
which group we mean. We also pick a particular dihedral subgroup for
ease of presentation; its rotation and reflection generators are,
respectively,

r={\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}}\text{-rotation around $\texttt{z}$-axis;\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,}p=\pi\text{-rotation around $\texttt{y}$-axis.}

(126)

As with the dihedral group, diagonal operators on
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
consist
of irrep-matrix elements of the group. The irreps are indexed by the total
angular momentum
\ell\geq 0
, and each irrep matrix
U_{\ell}
is
of dimension
2\ell+1
. The corresponding operators
\widehat{U}_{\ell}

satisfy the
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
analogue of Eq. (
14
),

\widehat{U}_{\ell}=\int_{\textnormal{{SO}}(3)}\text{d}R\,U_{\ell}(R)\,\left|R\right\rangle\left\langle R\right|\,,

(127)

where
\text{d}R
is the Haar measure satisfying
\int_{\textnormal{{SO}}(3)}\text{d}R=8\pi^{2}
.
Since
U_{\ell>0}
is a matrix, the above acts on both the internal irrep space and the
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
site space [see Eq. (
14
)].

Our embedding of dihedral operators

–

\widehat{Z}

into
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
operators

\widehat{U}
relies on knowledge of how the
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
irreps decompose or
branch

into
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
irreps. The defining irrep branches as
\ell=1\downarrow\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}\oplus\boldsymbol{2}_{1}
,
yielding the sign and defining two-dimensional irreps of
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
.
In terms of operators, this means that
\widehat{U}_{\ell=1}
, when acting on
basis vectors in
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
, branches into a block matrix of the

\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}
- and
\boldsymbol{2}_{1}
-irrep dihedral
Z
-operators,

\widehat{U}_{1}\left|R\right\rangle=\left[\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}}\oplus\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\boldsymbol{2}_{1}}\right]\left|R\right\rangle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{for}\,R\in\textnormal{{D}}_{N}\,.

(128)

To showcase the branching property, let us use the standard angular momentum basis
[Ref.
[
122
]
, Eq. (3.24)] for
U_{\ell}
and recall our choice
of dihedral group (
126
). Then,

U_{1}(r)=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}e^{i\frac{2\pi}{N}}&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{N}}\end{array}\right]\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,U_{1}(p)=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&1\\
0&-1&0\\
1&0&0\end{array}\right]\,.

(129)

The middle entry corresponds to the
\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}
irrep of
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
,
where
r\to 1
and
p\to-1
, while the two-by-two matrix made up
of the four corners is precisely the
\boldsymbol{2}_{1}
irrep (
16
).
In the chosen basis,
U_{1}
is only a permutation away from the two-block decomposition in Eq. (
128
).

More generally,
U_{\ell}(r)
for
\ell\leq\left\lfloor N/2\right\rfloor

has diagonal entries
e^{\pm i\frac{2\pi}{N}\ell}
in its corners,
while
U_{\ell}(p)
has contains 1’s in its upper-right and lower-left
corners. These four corners thus form the
\lambda=\boldsymbol{2}_{\ell}
irrep
of
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
. On the operator level, this means that
\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{0.86108pt}{--}\cr$\widehat{Z}$\cr}}}_{\boldsymbol{2}_{n}}

descends from
\widehat{U}_{\ell=n}
for all
n
. For
N
even,
the extra 1D irreps
\boldsymbol{1}^{\pm}
can be similarly obtained.

Embedding the general
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
flux ladder yields an instance of the
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
flux ladder,

\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}^{\text{edge}}\to-\sum_{\mathsf{x}}\,\,\sum_{\lambda\in\text{irr}(\textnormal{{D}}_{N})}\textsc{Tr}\big[\text{C}_{\ell_{\lambda}}^{\text{edge}}.\widehat{U}_{\ell_{\lambda}}^{\dagger\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}.\widehat{U}_{\ell_{\lambda}}^{\left(\mathsf{x}+1\right)}\big]+\sum_{h\in\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}\text{f}_{h}^{\text{edge}}\overleftarrow{X}_{R=h}^{\left(\mathsf{x}\right)}\,.

(130)

All
\overrightarrow{X}_{R=h}
and
\overleftarrow{X}_{R=h}
are now rotations acting on an
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)

space at each site
\mathsf{x}
, where the dihedral elements
h
are represented
as orthogonal
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
matrices
R
following Eq. (
126
),
and
\text{f}_{h}^{\text{edge}}=\text{f}_{h}
. Each
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
irrep
\lambda
in the
two-body term has a corresponding
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
irrep
\ell_{\lambda}
which
branches into
\lambda
when restricted to
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
. For example,
we have seen above that the
\ell=1
irrep houses two dihedral irreps
(
\ell_{\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}}=\ell_{\boldsymbol{2}_{1}}=1
). The corresponding
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)

parameter matrix for
\ell=1
, written in the standard angular momentum
basis, is then

\text{C}_{\ell_{\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}}=1}^{\text{edge}}+\text{C}_{\ell_{\boldsymbol{2}}=1}^{\text{edge}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}[\text{C}_{\lambda=\boldsymbol{2}_{1}}]_{11}&0&[\text{C}_{\lambda=\boldsymbol{2}_{1}}]_{12}\\
0&\text{C}_{\lambda=\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}}&0\\
{}[\text{C}_{\lambda=\boldsymbol{2}_{1}}]_{21}&0&[\text{C}_{\lambda=\boldsymbol{2}_{1}}]_{22}\end{array}\right]\,,

(131)

where
[\text{C}_{\boldsymbol{2}_{1}}]_{mn}
are elements of the parameter matrix
\text{C}_{\lambda=\boldsymbol{2}_{1}}
of
\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}^{\text{edge}}
(
27
).
The remaining
\text{C}_{\lambda}
can be embedded similarly into angular momenta

\ell_{\lambda}\leq N
.

In order to make sure the
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
model (
130
)
remembers its dihedral origins, we need to provide a potential
\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}

that pins each site onto the dihedral subspace.
This potential can be obtained by projecting
\widehat{U}_{\ell=N}

onto the trivial dihedral irrep for which
z
-axis rotations by

\phi
are represented by
e^{-i\phi N}
,

\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}=\textsc{Tr}\big[\varPi_{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}.\widehat{U}_{N}\big]=\int_{\textnormal{{SO}}(3)}\text{d}R\,\,V^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}\left(R\right)\left|R\right\rangle\left\langle R\right|\,.

(132)

To show that
V^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}(R)
is maximized only at the
2N

coordinates of our embedded dihedral group,
we parametrize
R
using Euler angles
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
in the
ZYZ

convention, with
\alpha,\gamma\in[0,2\pi)
and
\beta\in[0,\pi]
.
The potential function is

V^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}\left(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\right)=\cos\left[N\left(\alpha+\gamma\right)\right]\cos^{2N}{\textstyle\frac{\beta}{2}}+\cos\left[N\left(\alpha-\gamma\right)\right]\sin^{2N}{\textstyle\frac{\beta}{2}}\,.

(133)

This “stabilizer”
[
49
]
is maximized at
\beta\in\{0,\pi\}
and
\alpha\pm\gamma
being a multiple of
2\pi/N
— precisely the
2N
dihedral coordinates from Eq. (
126
).

C.2
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
flux ladder to principal chiral model

The key feature of
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
that yields sensible continuum limits
is that all rotations can be written as exponentials of elements of
the angular momentum Lie algebra. For the
X
-operators,

\overrightarrow{X}_{R}=e^{-i\phi_{R}^{\texttt{a}}\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\overleftarrow{X}_{R}=e^{-i\phi_{R}^{\texttt{a}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}}\,,

(134)

where
\phi_{R}^{\texttt{a}}
with
\texttt{a}\in\{\texttt{x},\texttt{y},\texttt{z}\}
are the

axis-angle coordinates
of
R
(all repeated
typewriter
-font
indices are summed). The direction of the vector
\phi_{R}
is the
axis around which the rotation matrix
R
rotates, while the norm

\phi_{R}^{\texttt{a}}\phi_{R}^{\texttt{a}}\leq\pi
is the angle of rotation. For
our chosen dihedral embedding (
126
),
\phi_{R=r}=(0,0,{\textstyle{\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}}})

and
\phi_{R=p}=(0,\pi,0)
. Commuting
with each other, these respective triples obey the standard
[
122
]

commutation relations amongst themselves:
[\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}},\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{b}}]=i\epsilon_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{c}}
,
[\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}},\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{b}}]=i\epsilon_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{c}}
, and
[\overrightarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}},\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{b}}]=0
(with
\epsilon
the anti-symmetric
tensor).

The
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
irrep matrices
U_{\ell}
can also be written in terms
axis-angle coordinates
\phi^{\texttt{a}}
and their own
2\ell+1
-dimensional
generators
L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}}
,

U_{\ell}\left(R\right)=e^{-i\phi_{R}^{\texttt{a}}L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}}}\,,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{where}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,[L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}},L_{\ell}^{\texttt{b}}]=i\epsilon_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}L_{\ell}^{\texttt{c}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textsc{Tr}\big[L_{\ell}^{\texttt{a}}.L_{\ell}^{\texttt{b}}\big]=\delta_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}c_{\ell}\,.

(135)

We continue to use the standard angular momentum
basis for these generators, with the normalization constant
c_{\ell}=\frac{1}{3}\ell\left(\ell+1\right)\left(2\ell+1\right)
.

Performing all expansions, assuming that
\text{C}^{\text{edge}}
are Hermitian
and
\text{f}^{\text{edge}}
are real, and adding a pinning term yield a generalized

\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
principal chiral model

\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}^{\text{edge}}\to\int\text{d}\mathsf{x}\,\,\text{C}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\,\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{a}}\!\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}^{\texttt{b}}\!\left(\mathsf{x}\right)+\text{f}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}\!\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{b}}\!\left(\mathsf{x}\right)-\text{M}\textsc{Tr}\big[\varPi_{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}.\widehat{U}_{N}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\big]~.

(136)

The constituent fields are the
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
-valued
position
\widehat{U}
(
127
), position current
\overleftarrow{\partial\phi}\propto\widehat{U}^{\dagger}\partial\widehat{U}

(
79
), and “right” momentum current

\overleftarrow{L}
(
134
). The last term (
132
)
pins the
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
space to
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
in the
\text{M}\to\infty
limit.
The parameters
\{\text{C}_{\ell}^{\text{edge}},\text{f}_{h}^{\text{edge}}\}
of the flux
ladder (
130
) are absorbed into matrices
\text{C}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}

and
\text{f}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}
, shown explicitly for this
General-edge

case in Table
4
.

In generalizing
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
to other
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
, the
\epsilon_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}

in the defining (
77
) commutation relations should be replaced
by the structure constants
f_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}
defining
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
’s Lie
algebra. Expanding a uniform sum of
\overleftarrow{X}
’s in the principal chiral
model (
130
) is generally ambiguous because the
coordinates
\phi_{g}^{\texttt{a}}
specifying elements
g\in\textnormal{{G}}
inside
the manifold
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
are not unique. A unique expansion can be obtained
by averaging the
X
-term over
G
before expansion,

\sum_{g\in\textnormal{{G}}}e^{-i\phi_{g}^{\texttt{a}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}}={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{g\in\textnormal{{G}}}e^{-i\phi_{g}^{\texttt{a}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{g\in\textnormal{{G}}}e^{-i\phi_{hgh^{-1}}^{\texttt{a}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}}={\textstyle\frac{1}{\left|\textnormal{{G}}\right|}}\sum_{g,h\in\textnormal{{G}}}e^{-i(R_{h}\phi_{g})^{\texttt{a}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}}\to\text{f}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{b}}\,,

(137)

where
\overleftarrow{L}
are the generators of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
’s Lie algebra, we have used
the adjoint representation (
83
) in writing
\phi_{hgh^{-1}}^{\texttt{a}}=R_{h}^{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\phi_{g}^{\texttt{b}}=(R_{h}\phi_{g})^{\texttt{a}}
,
and

\text{f}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}={\textstyle\frac{1}{2\left|\textnormal{{G}}\right|}}\sum_{g,h\in\textnormal{{G}}}(R_{h}\phi_{g})^{\texttt{a}}(R_{h}\phi_{g})^{\texttt{b}}\,.

(138)

This averaging twirls the sum over the possible coordinate realizations,
guaranteeing that
\text{f}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{a}}\overleftarrow{L}^{\texttt{b}}
is invariant under

\overleftarrow{X}_{a}^{\textsf{(global)}}
for any
a\in\textnormal{{G}}
. This was not necessary for
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}=\textnormal{{SO}}(3)

due to the uniqueness of the angle-axis representation when
\phi_{g}^{\texttt{a}}\phi_{g}^{\texttt{a}}

is minimized.

Appendix D
Continuum description of the quantum-double bulk

We apply the embedding and pinning procedure from Sec.
5

to the surface-code (
3
) and quantum-double (
18
)
bulk Hamiltonians. We recover, respectively, an abelian gauge theory
and a non-Abelian Yang-Mills gauge theory
[
52
,
82
,
100
,
101
]
.
Both are known to contain non-Abelian defects analogous to the anyons
of the respective lattice models
[
102
,
103
,
104
,
105
,
106
]
.

D.1
Surface code to abelian gauge theory

Here, we apply the procedure from Sec.
5
to convert
the qunit surface code (
3
) to a
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
-pinned
abelian gauge field theory. We first perform the well-known
[
52
,
82
]

relabeling required for the 2D continuum limit. This relabeling produces
a pair of fields, each corresponding to one of the two dimensions
of the lattice. Operators on the top and bottom of a plaquette are
identified as the field
\widehat{\phi}_{\mathsf{1}}

\displaystyle\to\widehat{G}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)=\partial_{\mathsf{1}}\widehat{L}_{\mathsf{1}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)+\partial_{\mathsf{2}}\widehat{L}_{\mathsf{2}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\,.

(139b)

The
\widehat{F}
operator is known as the curvature of
gauge potential
\widehat{\phi}
, and
\widehat{G}
is a generator of local gauge transformations.
Assuming all fields vary smoothly and slowly and expanding both plaquettes
and stars to second order yields the gauge field theory

\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}^{\text{bulk}}\to\int\text{d}^{2}\mathsf{x}\,\,\text{C}\,\widehat{F}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\widehat{F}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)+\text{f}\,\widehat{G}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\widehat{G}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)-\text{M}\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\,,

(140)

with parameters
\text{C}={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{\lambda=1}^{N-1}\lambda^{2}
and
\text{f}={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{h=1}^{N-1}\left({\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{N}}h\right)^{2}
,
and with
\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)
consisting of one cosine for each
\widehat{\phi}_{\mathsf{1}}

and
\widehat{\phi}_{\mathsf{2}}
. This 2D bulk field theory is similar to the
1D edge field theory (
64
) in that it is
quadratic in two “dual” fields. However, there are also key differences
in the field algebras: (I) the 1D fields
\{\partial\widehat{\phi},\widehat{L}\}
do not quite
commute (
95
), while the commutator of the
2D fields
\{\widehat{F},\widehat{G}\}
is zero; and (II) the 1D symmetry generator

\widehat{L}^{\textsf{(global)}}=\int d\mathsf{x}\,\widehat{L}(\mathsf{x})
shifts
\widehat{\phi}
, while the 2D gauge-transformation
generator
\widehat{G}^{\textsf{(global)}}=\int\text{d}\mathsf{x}\,\widehat{G}(\mathsf{x})
commutes with each
\widehat{\phi}_{\mathsf{i}}
.
These imply all terms in this 2D theory are
\textnormal{{U}}(1)
symmetric. The

\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
symmetry comes about when
\text{M}\to\infty
, in which case

\widehat{\phi}_{\mathsf{i}}
are pinned to multiplies of
\frac{2\pi}{N}
.

Does the above continuum limit preserve key features of the surface-code lattice model? It turns out that topological defects of the pure
gauge theory (
\widehat{F}^{2}
and
\widehat{G}^{2}
terms above) are in fact classified
by the same data as the anyonic excitations of the surface code,
when

the
\textnormal{{U}}(1)
gauge symmetry is broken to a
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
subgroup. A
mechanism (
[
106
]
, Sec. 1.3) for identifying such excitations
proceeds by (A) adding a matter or Higgs field
\widehat{\varPhi}
(which transforms
as
\widehat{\varPhi}\to e^{iN}\widehat{\varPhi}
under the gauge symmetry), (B) using
\widehat{\varPhi}

to spontaneously break said symmetry to
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
via the Higgs mechanism
(see
[
122
]
, Sec. 18.4), and (C) constructing long-range
interacting electric charges and magnetic vortices (topological defects)
out of combinations of the gauge and Goldstone fields. In our theory (
140
), the symmetry is broken
using the gauge potential
itself
: the cosine potentials (as

\text{M}\to\infty
) ensure that
\widehat{\phi}_{\mathsf{i}}
are pinned to
\textnormal{{Z}}_{N}
, which
in turn should constrain the
\widehat{F}
and
\widehat{G}
operators (
139
)
in similar fashion.

D.2

0&1&0\\
0&0&2\end{array}\right]\,.

for the
X
-type field.
The key difference from the abelian case is the presence of two momentum
generators,
\overrightarrow{L}
and
\overleftarrow{L}
. Since they are related via the equivalence
(
102
), one can obtain the continuum
version of that equivalence by recalling that matrix elements of the
generators
l^{\texttt{a}}
of the adjoint rotation
R
correspond to the
antisymmetric tensor
\epsilon_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}
[
[
122
]
, Eq. (4.3)],

\overleftarrow{L}_{\mathsf{j}}^{\texttt{a}}=-\widehat{R^{-1}}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\overrightarrow{L}_{\mathsf{j}}^{\texttt{b}}\approx-\overrightarrow{L}_{\mathsf{j}}^{\texttt{a}}-i\widehat{\phi}^{\texttt{c}}[l^{\texttt{c}}]_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\overrightarrow{L}_{\mathsf{j}}^{\texttt{b}}=-\overrightarrow{L}_{\mathsf{j}}^{\texttt{a}}-i\epsilon_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}\widehat{\phi}^{\texttt{b}}\overrightarrow{L}_{\mathsf{j}}^{\texttt{c}}\,.

(143)

Taking the continuum limit, renaming
\overrightarrow{L}\to\widehat{L}
, and plugging this
into the star term, the plaquette and star terms become exponentials
of the following two respective fields,

\displaystyle\widehat{F}^{\texttt{a}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mathsf{1}}\widehat{\phi}_{\mathsf{2}}^{\texttt{a}}-\partial_{\mathsf{2}}\widehat{\phi}_{\mathsf{1}}^{\texttt{a}}-i\epsilon_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}\widehat{\phi}_{\mathsf{1}}^{\texttt{b}}\widehat{\phi}_{\mathsf{2}}^{\texttt{c}}

(144a)

\displaystyle\widehat{G}^{\texttt{a}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mathsf{1}}\widehat{L}_{\mathsf{1}}^{\texttt{a}}+\partial_{\mathsf{2}}\widehat{L}_{\mathsf{2}}^{\texttt{a}}-i\epsilon_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}\texttt{c}}\left(\widehat{\phi}_{\mathsf{1}}^{\texttt{b}}\widehat{L}_{\mathsf{1}}^{\texttt{c}}+\widehat{\phi}_{\mathsf{2}}^{\texttt{b}}\widehat{L}_{\mathsf{2}}^{\texttt{c}}\right)\,.

(144b)

The field
\widehat{F}
is the curvature of the
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)

gauge potential, while
\widehat{G}
generate
\textnormal{{SO}}(3)
gauge transformations.
The commutation relations between the site degrees of freedom
\{\widehat{\phi}_{\mathsf{j}},\widehat{L}_{\mathsf{j}}\}

and these gauge fields are calculated in Table
7
.
As with the 1D case in Sec.
6.4
,
derivatives of
\delta
arise, but remarkably cancel to yield the correct
behavior of
\widehat{F}
and
\widehat{G}
under gauge transformations.

Expanding the star and plaquette terms yields the
\textnormal{{D}}_{N}
-pinned
Yang-Mills theory

\widehat{H}_{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}^{\text{bulk}}\to\int\text{d}^{2}\mathsf{x}\,\,\,\text{C}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\widehat{F}^{\texttt{a}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\widehat{F}^{\texttt{b}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)+\text{f}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\widehat{G}^{\texttt{a}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\widehat{G}^{\texttt{b}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)-\text{M}\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}\left(\mathsf{x}\right)\,,

(145)

with parameters
\{\text{C}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}},\text{f}_{\texttt{a}\texttt{b}}\}
identical to those
in the second line of Table
4
, but with
\text{edge}\to\text{bulk}
.
The potential
\widehat{V}^{\text{smooth}}_{\textnormal{{D}}_{N}}
(
132
)
pins both
\widehat{\phi}_{\mathsf{1}}^{\texttt{a}}
and
\widehat{\phi}_{\mathsf{2}}^{\texttt{a}}
. The quadratic
terms of this theory, under the Higgs mechanism, are known
[
106
]

to admit topological defects with properties identical to the quantum
double anyons of the lattice model (
18
).
